<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01RdbRTq5CHqa62mvqByVEPT`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	
3	\subsection{Execution Traces for Code LLMs}
4	
5	Recent work has scaled trace-augmented training: TracePile \citep{chen2025tracepile} pre-trained on 2.6M execution-annotated samples, while \citet{liu2023codeexecution} pre-trained models to predict execution outputs from traces.
6	\citet{wang2025dcsh} found trace utility to be task- and format-dependent but did not decompose individual trace components; relative to their work, we contribute a two-tier signal hierarchy, a phase transition in trace-length budgets, calibration analysis, and cross-architecture validation.
7	NExT \citep{ni2024next} and ET-CoT \citep{eguchi2025etcot} leveraged trace chain-of-thought for execution reasoning, and \citet{ferrando2025execute} found compact traces comparable to full traces, aligning with our density finding.
8	CRUXEval \citep{gu2024cruxeval}, extended to 19 languages by CRUXEval-X \citep{xu2025cruxevalx}, established execution reasoning benchmarks, though neither decomposes which trace components contribute to \emph{classification} accuracy.
9	\citet{shi2023distracted} showed LLMs are easily distracted by irrelevant context, providing a complementary explanation for why verbose traces impair SFT learning.
10	In the prompt compression literature, LLMLingua \citep{jiang2023llmlingua} and Selective Context \citep{li2023selectivecontext} showed that pruning low-information tokens preserves downstream accuracy; TraceCoder \citep{huang2026tracecoder} and InspectCoder \citep{wang2025inspectcoder} apply selective trace extraction for automated debugging; and \citet{haque2025traces} found raw traces provide limited repair benefit.
11	Our density decomposition complements these methods by quantifying \emph{why} compression helps (gradient dilution explains ${\leq}$24\% of the gap; the remainder reflects inference-time interference).
12	
13	\begin{table}[t]
14	\centering
15	\caption{Cross-model verdict accuracy (\%, $N{=}1{,}190$) across four models (1.5B--13B) and three architecture families. Multi-seed entries report mean$\pm$std over 2--3 seeds. Bold: best per model. Full per-seed breakdowns in Appendix~\ref{sec:full_results}; per-class metrics in Appendix~\ref{sec:per_class}.}
16	\label{tab:cross_model}
17	\small
18	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
19	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
20	\toprule
21	\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Qwen-7B} & \textbf{Qwen-1.5B} & \textbf{CL-13B} & \textbf{DS-6.7B} \\
22	\midrule
23	exception\_only & \textbf{99.83}{\scriptsize$\pm$0.00} & \textbf{99.83}{\scriptsize$\pm$0.00} & \textbf{99.89}{\scriptsize$\pm$0.10} & \textbf{99.39}{\scriptsize$\pm$0.10} \\
24	ae\_only & 99.64{\scriptsize$\pm$0.49} & 98.83{\scriptsize$\pm$0.36} & 99.08{\scriptsize$\pm$0.00} & 98.53{\scriptsize$\pm$0.30} \\
25	VOTA & 99.05{\scriptsize$\pm$0.38} & 99.54{\scriptsize$\pm$0.06} & 98.74{\scriptsize$\pm$0.24} & 99.25$^\ddag$ \\
26	loop\_only & 86.05{\scriptsize$\pm$0.52} & 79.54{\scriptsize$\pm$1.42} & 77.40{\scriptsize$\pm$1.54} & 80.31{\scriptsize$\pm$1.65} \\
27	no\_trace & 75.13 & 72.35{\scriptsize$\pm$4.48} & 76.47 & 73.36 \\

32	}
33	\vspace{2pt}
34	\par\raggedright\footnotesize
35	DeepSeek \cond{exception\_only} 3 seeds: 99.33/99.50/99.33 ($\sigma{=}$0.10pp).
36	VOTA on Qwen-7B: 99.41/98.66/99.08 ($\sigma{=}$0.38).
37	\cond{ae\_only} 3 seeds: 99.92/99.08/99.92 (mean 99.64\%); Qwen-7B \cond{loop\_only} 3 seeds: 85.63/85.88/86.64 (mean 86.05\%).
38	Qwen-1.5B \cond{exception\_only}: 99.83\% across 3 seeds ($\sigma{=}$0).
39	$^\ddag$DeepSeek VOTA 3 seeds: 84.45/99.16/99.33; seed 42 (84.45\%, fail recall\,=\,45.4\%) is a training failure due to tokenizer-driven truncation (Appendix~\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}), reported as 2-seed mean.
40	\end{table}
41	
42	\subsection{Verdict Prediction and Fault Detection}
43	
44	Verdict prediction is an instance of the longstanding test oracle problem \citep{barr2015oracle}.
45	TRACED \citep{ding2024traced} showed execution-aware pretraining improves vulnerability detection; DeepDFA \citep{steenhoek2024deepdfa} and learned program slicing \citep{yadavally2024slicing} combined classical program analysis with deep learning for defect detection.
46	In patch correctness prediction, representation learning of code changes has progressed from CC2Vec \citep{hoang2020cc2vec} to LLM4PatchCorrect \citep{zhou2024llm4patch} (84.4\%) and APPT \citep{appt2024} (79.7\%); \citet{tian2020patchcorrectness} evaluated change representations for predicting repair correctness.
47	FuseFL \citep{widyasari2024fusefl} and \citet{foster2025mutation} demonstrated that structured fault signals (stack traces, mutation outcomes) improve localization; the equivalent-mutant problem \citep{papadakis2019mutation} poses an analogous classification challenge.
48	Spectrum-based fault localization (SBFL) ranks program elements by suspiciousness from test spectra \citep{abreu2007accuracy,jones2002visualization,wong2014dstar}; recent LLM-based approaches such as AutoFL \citep{kang2024autofl} and AgentFL \citep{qin2024agentfl} share our insight that anomalous execution events carry the strongest diagnostic signal.
49	In agent-based repair, RepairAgent \citep{bouzenia2025repairagent}, Agentless \citep{xia2024agentless}, SWE-agent \citep{yang2024sweagent}, and OpenHands \citep{wang2025openhands} all incorporate execution feedback in repair workflows; in the SWE-bench ecosystem \citep{jimenez2024swebench}, these agents rely on test verdict signals to guide patch selection, yet which trace components carry that signal most efficiently remains unstudied.
50	LDB \citep{zhong2024ldb} applies segment-level trace verification for debugging, and SelfDebug \citep{chen2024selfdebug} and Reflexion \citep{shinn2023reflexion} iteratively refine code using execution outcomes.
51	None of these works isolate individual trace components or compare against rule-based baselines.
52	
53	\subsection{Calibration and Training Dynamics}
54	
55	Neural network calibration \citep{guo2017calibration}, extended to LLMs \citep{kadavath2022language,geng2024calibration}, studies confidence--correctness alignment; \citet{spiess2025calibration} specifically addressed calibration for code models.
56	Our ECE ${<}$ 0.007 across four models suggests compact inputs improve calibration beyond accuracy gains alone.
57	Just-in-time defect prediction \citep{kamei2013large,hoang2019deepjit,pornprasit2021jitline} shares our fault detection motivation but operates on code change features rather than execution traces; our work complements this line by showing that execution-time fault signals can be classified with near-perfect accuracy when properly extracted.
58	Curriculum learning \citep{bengio2009curriculum}, dataset cartography via training dynamics \citep{swayamdipta2020cartography}, and data selection methods \citep{sachdeva2024dataefficient,xie2023doremi} demonstrate that data quality and ordering improve convergence---consistent with our finding that compact exceptions enable convergence within 50 steps while verbose traces trap the model in oscillation across all 444 training steps.
59	\citet{yang2024generalization} further showed that fine-tuned LLMs generalize better on concise inputs, providing a complementary perspective on why information density governs SFT effectiveness.
60	
```

relative to their work, we contribute a two-tier signal hierarchy, a phase transition in trace-length budgets, calibration analysis, and cross-architecture validation.\nNExT \\citep{ni2024next} and ET-CoT \\citep{eguchi2025etcot} leveraged trace chain-of-thought for execution reasoning, and \\citet{ferrando2025execute} found compact traces comparable to full traces, aligning with our density finding.\nCRUXEval \\citep{gu2024cruxeval}, extended to 19 languages by CRUXEval-X \\citep{xu2025cruxevalx}, established execution reasoning benchmarks, though neither decomposes which trace components contribute to \\emph{classification} accuracy.\n\\citet{shi2023distracted} showed LLMs are easily distracted by irrelevant context, providing a complementary explanation for why verbose traces impair SFT learning.\nIn the prompt compression literature, LLMLingua \\citep{jiang2023llmlingua} and Selective Context \\citep{li2023selectivecontext} showed that pruning low-information tokens preserves downstream accuracy; TraceCoder \\citep{huang2026tracecoder} and InspectCoder \\citep{wang2025inspectcoder} apply selective trace extraction for automated debugging; and \\citet{haque2025traces} found raw traces provide limited repair benefit.\nOur density decomposition complements these methods by quantifying \\emph{why} compression helps (gradient dilution explains ${\\leq}$24\\% of the gap; the remainder reflects inference-time interference).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-model verdict accuracy (\\%, $N{=}1{,}190$) across four models (1.5B--13B) and three architecture families. Multi-seed entries report mean$\\pm$std over 2--3 seeds. Bold: best per model. Full per-seed breakdowns in Appendix~\\ref{sec:full_results}; per-class metrics in Appendix~\\ref{sec:per_class}.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Qwen-7B} & \\textbf{Qwen-1.5B} & \\textbf{CL-13B} & \\textbf{DS-6.7B} \\\\\n\\midrule\nexception\\_only & \\textbf{99.83}{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.00} & \\textbf{99.83}{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.00} & \\textbf{99.89}{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.10} & \\textbf{99.39}{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.10} \\\\\nae\\_only & 99.64{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.49} & 98.83{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.36} & 99.08{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.00} & 98.53{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.30} \\\\\nVOTA & 99.05{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.38} & 99.54{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.06} & 98.74{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.24} & 99.25$^\\ddag$ \\\\\nloop\\_only & 86.05{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.52} & 79.54{\\scriptsize$\\pm$1.42} & 77.40{\\scriptsize$\\pm$1.54} & 80.31{\\scriptsize$\\pm$1.65} \\\\\nno\\_trace & 75.13 & 72.35{\\scriptsize$\\pm$4.48} & 76.47 & 73.36 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMajority baseline & \\multicolumn{4}{c}{71.51} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\par\\raggedright\\footnotesize\nDeepSeek \\cond{exception\\_only} 3 seeds: 99.33/99.50/99.33 ($\\sigma{=}$0.10pp).\nVOTA on Qwen-7B: 99.41/98.66/99.08 ($\\sigma{=}$0.38).\n\\cond{ae\\_only} 3 seeds: 99.92/99.08/99.92 (mean 99.64\\%); Qwen-7B \\cond{loop\\_only} 3 seeds: 85.63/85.88/86.64 (mean 86.05\\%).\nQwen-1.5B \\cond{exception\\_only}: 99.83\\% across 3 seeds ($\\sigma{=}$0).\n$^\\ddag$DeepSeek VOTA 3 seeds: 84.45/99.16/99.33; seed 42 (84.45\\%, fail recall\\,=\\,45.4\\%) is a training failure due to tokenizer-driven truncation (Appendix~\\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}), reported as 2-seed mean.\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Verdict Prediction and Fault Detection}\n\nVerdict prediction is an instance of the longstanding test oracle problem \\citep{barr2015oracle}.\nTRACED \\citep{ding2024traced} showed execution-aware pretraining improves vulnerability detection; DeepDFA \\citep{steenhoek2024deepdfa} and learned program slicing \\citep{yadavally2024slicing} combined classical program analysis with deep learning for defect detection.\nIn patch correctness prediction, representation learning of code changes has progressed from CC2Vec \\citep{hoang2020cc2vec} to LLM4PatchCorrect \\citep{zhou2024llm4patch} (84.4\\%) and APPT \\citep{appt2024} (79.7\\%); \\citet{tian2020patchcorrectness} evaluated change representations for predicting repair correctness.\nFuseFL \\citep{widyasari2024fusefl} and \\citet{foster2025mutation} demonstrated that structured fault signals (stack traces, mutation outcomes) improve localization; the equivalent-mutant problem \\citep{papadakis2019mutation} poses an analogous classification challenge.\nSpectrum-based fault localization (SBFL) ranks program elements by suspiciousness from test spectra \\citep{abreu2007accuracy,jones2002visualization,wong2014dstar}; recent LLM-based approaches such as AutoFL \\citep{kang2024autofl} and AgentFL \\citep{qin2024agentfl} share our insight that anomalous execution events carry the strongest diagnostic signal.\nIn agent-based repair, RepairAgent \\citep{bouzenia2025repairagent}, Agentless \\citep{xia2024agentless}, SWE-agent \\citep{yang2024sweagent}, and OpenHands \\citep{wang2025openhands} all incorporate execution feedback in repair workflows; in the SWE-bench ecosystem \\citep{jimenez2024swebench}, these agents rely on test verdict signals to guide patch selection, yet which trace components carry that signal most efficiently remains unstudied.\nLDB \\citep{zhong2024ldb} applies segment-level trace verification for debugging, and SelfDebug \\citep{chen2024selfdebug} and Reflexion \\citep{shinn2023reflexion} iteratively refine code using execution outcomes.\nNone of these works isolate individual trace components or compare against rule-based baselines.\n\n\\subsection{Calibration and Training Dynamics}\n\nNeural network calibration \\citep{guo2017calibration}, extended to LLMs \\citep{kadavath2022language,geng2024calibration}, studies confidence--correctness alignment; \\citet{spiess2025calibration} specifically addressed calibration for code models.\nOur ECE ${<}$ 0.007 across four models suggests compact inputs improve calibration beyond accuracy gains alone.\nJust-in-time defect prediction \\citep{kamei2013large,hoang2019deepjit,pornprasit2021jitline} shares our fault detection motivation but operates on code change features rather than execution traces; our work complements this line by showing that execution-time fault signals can be classified with near-perfect accuracy when properly extracted.\nCurriculum learning \\citep{bengio2009curriculum}, dataset cartography via training dynamics \\citep{swayamdipta2020cartography}, and data selection methods \\citep{sachdeva2024dataefficient,xie2023doremi} demonstrate that data quality and ordering improve convergence---consistent with our finding that compact exceptions enable convergence within 50 steps while verbose traces trap the model in oscillation across all 444 training steps.\n\\citet{yang2024generalization} further showed that fine-tuned LLMs generalize better on concise inputs, providing a complementary perspective on why information density governs SFT effectiveness.\n\n",

\\citet{tian2020patchcorrectness} evaluated change representations for predicting repair correctness.\nFuseFL \\citep{widyasari2024fusefl} and \\citet{foster2025mutation} demonstrated that structured fault signals (stack traces, mutation outcomes) improve localization; the equivalent-mutant problem \\citep{papadakis2019mutation} poses an analogous classification challenge.\nSpectrum-based fault localization (SBFL) ranks program elements by suspiciousness from test spectra \\citep{abreu2007accuracy,jones2002visualization,wong2014dstar}; recent LLM-based approaches such as AutoFL \\citep{kang2024autofl} and AgentFL \\citep{qin2024agentfl} share our insight that anomalous execution events carry the strongest diagnostic signal.\nIn agent-based repair, RepairAgent \\citep{bouzenia2025repairagent}, Agentless \\citep{xia2024agentless}, SWE-agent \\citep{yang2024sweagent}, and OpenHands \\citep{wang2025openhands} all incorporate execution feedback in repair workflows; in the SWE-bench ecosystem \\citep{jimenez2024swebench}, these agents rely on test verdict signals to guide patch selection, yet which trace components carry that signal most efficiently remains unstudied.\nLDB \\citep{zhong2024ldb} applies segment-level trace verification for debugging, and SelfDebug \\citep{chen2024selfdebug} and Reflexion \\citep{shinn2023reflexion} iteratively refine code using execution outcomes.\nNone of these works isolate individual trace components or compare against rule-based baselines.\n\n\\subsection{Calibration and Training Dynamics}\n\nNeural network calibration \\citep{guo2017calibration}, extended to LLMs \\citep{kadavath2022language,geng2024calibration}, studies confidence--correctness alignment; \\citet{spiess2025calibration} specifically addressed calibration for code models.\nOur ECE ${<}$ 0.007 across four models suggests compact inputs improve calibration beyond accuracy gains alone.\nJust-in-time defect prediction \\citep{kamei2013large,hoang2019deepjit,pornprasit2021jitline} shares our fault detection motivation but operates on code change features rather than execution traces; our work complements this line by showing that execution-time fault signals can be classified with near-perfect accuracy when properly extracted.\nCurriculum learning \\citep{bengio2009curriculum}, dataset cartography via training dynamics \\citep{swayamdipta2020cartography}, and data selection methods \\citep{sachdeva2024dataefficient,xie2023doremi} demonstrate that data quality and ordering improve convergence---consistent with our finding that compact exceptions enable convergence within 50 steps while verbose traces trap the model in oscillation across all 444 training steps.\n\\citet{yang2024generalization} further showed that fine-tuned LLMs generalize better on concise inputs, providing a complementary perspective on why information density governs SFT effectiveness.\n\n",

```json
    "newString": "Improvement over standard \\cond{full\\_trace} is attributable to backward-pass signal dilution; the remaining gap to VOTA reflects inference-time input complexity.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Component ablation on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$). Tier~1 signals achieve near-perfect accuracy; combining them (VOTA) slightly dilutes performance. Entries marked $^\\dag$/$^\\S$/$^\\ddag$ are 3-seed mean$\\pm$std; per-seed breakdowns in Appendix~\\ref{sec:full_results}. Seed $\\sigma$ negatively correlates with density (0.00 for exception to 0.52 for loop).}\n\\label{tab:ablation}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Components} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{Tier} \\\\\n\\midrule\nae\\_only & assertion-point & 99.64$^\\dag$ & [99.26, 99.87] & 1 \\\\\nexception\\_only & exception events & \\textbf{99.83} & [99.58, 100.00] & 1 \\\\\nVOTA & exc+ae+loop & 99.05$^\\S$ & [98.49, 99.50] & --- \\\\\nVOTA\\_no\\_ae & exc+loop & 99.41 & [98.90, 99.80] & --- \\\\\nloop\\_only & loop compression & 86.05$^\\ddag$ & [83.97, 87.96] & 2 \\\\\nfull\\_trace & complete trace & 81.76 & [79.70, 83.90] & --- \\\\\nno\\_trace & none & 75.13 & [72.77, 77.48] & --- \\\\\n\\midrule\nRegex (best) & exc.\\ type regex & 93.61 & --- & --- \\\\\n3-shot (Qwen-7B) & in-context learning & 90.84 & --- & --- \\\\\nZero-shot (4 models) & prompting & 51.0--93.5 & --- & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\n\\subsection{Task Definition}\n\nWe study \\emph{verdict prediction}: given a Python function, a test input, and an optional execution trace, predict whether the test passes or fails.\nThe model produces a single token (\\textit{pass} or \\textit{fail}), making this a binary classification task.\nUnlike code generation \\citep{chen2025tracepile} or output prediction \\citep{gu2024cruxeval}, verdict prediction isolates the model's ability to detect faults from its ability to simulate execution.\nThe discrete binary outcome makes the effect of each trace component directly measurable, providing cleaner ablation contrasts than generation tasks where output quality varies continuously.\n\n\\subsection{Dataset}\n\\label{sec:dataset}\n\nWe sampled 10 candidate solutions per problem from Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct (temperature 0.8, top\\_p 0.95) for 591 problems from HumanEval \\citep{chen2021evaluating} (164) and MBPP-sanitized \\citep{austin2021program} (427), executed each against canonical tests with \\textit{sys.settrace} to obtain line-level traces and pass/fail verdicts, yielding 5,910 pairs (67\\% pass, 33\\% fail).\nThe dataset is split 80/20 by problem ID into 4,720 training and 1,190 test samples (majority baseline: 71.51\\%).\nThe \\cond{exception\\_only} format collapses passing solutions into identical rows (39.45\\% train, 48.99\\% test duplicates); these duplicates arise from multiple candidate solutions per problem, with the train--test split enforced at the problem level (472 train / 119 test problems, zero crossover; Appendix~\\ref{sec:dedup_stats}).\n\n\\subsection{Trace Representations}\n\nWe define seven conditions that vary the trace information available.\n\\cond{no\\_trace} provides only source code and test input.\n\\cond{full\\_trace} includes the complete line-by-line trace with variable states.\n\\cond{VOTA} combines exception events, assertion-point comparisons, and loop compression (Appendix~\\ref{sec:vota_algorithm}).\nThe remaining conditions isolate individual VOTA components:\n\\cond{exception\\_only} retains only exception and error events;\n\\cond{ae\\_only} retains only assertion-point actual/expected pairs;\n\\cond{loop\\_only} retains only loop compression summaries.\n\\cond{VOTA\\_no\\_ae} is VOTA without assertion-point information.\n\n\\subsection{Models and Training}\n\nWe evaluate four models spanning three architecture families: \\textbf{Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B/1.5B-Instruct} \\citep{qwen2025coder}, \\textbf{CodeLlama-13B-Instruct} \\citep{roziere2024code}, and \\textbf{DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct} \\citep{deepseek2024coder}, covering a 1.5B--13B parameter range (8.7$\\times$).\nAll conditions use LoRA \\citep{hu2022lora} (rank=16, $\\alpha$=32, dropout=0.05) on all linear layers, 3 epochs, effective batch size 32, LR $2 \\times 10^{-4}$ with linear warmup/decay, max sequence length 2{,}048 tokens.\nSequences exceeding this limit are truncated while preserving the verdict label.\nThe primary seed is 42; seeds 123 and 456 test \\cond{exception\\_only} robustness (Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_details}).\n\n\\subsection{Loss Ablation}\n\nTo disentangle training signal from input signal quality, we introduce \\cond{full\\_trace\\_label\\_only}: identical full-trace input but loss computed only on the verdict token (trace positions masked).\nImprovement over standard \\cond{full\\_trace} is attributable to backward-pass signal dilution; the remaining gap to VOTA reflects inference-time input complexity.\n",
```

```json
        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\nWe evaluate two questions:\n(1)~Do exception signals generalize across model architectures and scales?\n(2)~What is the relative contribution of each trace component?\nAll experiments use $N{=}1{,}190$ test samples with Wilson 95\\% CIs.\\footnote{Data is partitioned at the problem level (472 train / 119 test problems, zero crossover; \\S\\ref{sec:dataset}). The \\cond{exception\\_only} format collapses passing solutions into identical rows (48.99\\% test duplicates; Appendix~\\ref{sec:dedup_stats}), but these arise from within-problem variants, not cross-set leakage; deduplication sensitivity analysis confirms 0.00pp delta across all 19 experimental conditions.}\nAll cross-tier differences are significant at $p{<}.0001$ (paired bootstrap, $B{=}10{,}000$) unless noted.\nUnder problem-level clustered bootstrap (effective $N{\\approx}119$), cross-tier gaps remain significant ($p{<}.001$); within-tier comparisons show partially overlapping CIs.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Comparison}\n\\label{sec:cross_model}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_model} compares four models spanning three architecture families across a 1.5B--13B parameter range.\n\n\\paragraph{Exception dominance across families.}\nUnder \\cond{exception\\_only}, all four models converge to ${\\geq}$99.33\\% across all seeds, with a cross-model range of just 0.50pp---the tightest of any condition.\nCodeLlama-13B reaches 100\\% on one seed, confirming that the two persistent errors on Qwen (both \\textit{GeneratorExit}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:irreducible}) are convergence artifacts rather than hard data limitations.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale invariance.}\nException accuracy is independent of model scale: the smallest model (1.5B) matches the largest (13B) on the same representation (Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_scale}).\nIn contrast, \\cond{no\\_trace} scales monotonically with capacity and exhibits the highest seed variance of any condition.\nException events eliminate both the scale gap and the instability.\n\n\\paragraph{VOTA robustness.}\nExcluding one training failure caused by tokenizer-induced truncation (Appendix~\\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}), VOTA achieves ${\\geq}$98.57\\% across all models within a 0.80pp range.\nThe failure is itself informative: 91.7\\% of that seed's training samples were truncated, while compact \\cond{exception\\_only} inputs (median 150 tokens) sidestep truncation entirely.\n\n\\subsection{Component Ablation}\n\\label{sec:ablation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:ablation} decomposes VOTA on Qwen-2.5-7B, revealing a two-tier hierarchy.\nTier~1 signals are individually sufficient: \\cond{exception\\_only} alone exceeds full VOTA, implying that combining additional components introduces noise rather than value.\nRemoving assertion points has no effect (\\cond{VOTA\\_no\\_ae}), confirming exception dominance.\nThe Tier~1/2 boundary is sharp (Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot}): the weakest Tier~1 condition exceeds the strongest Tier~2 by over 12pp with non-overlapping confidence intervals.\nTier~2 (\\cond{loop\\_only}) benefits are model-dependent: Qwen-7B gains +10.92pp over \\cond{no\\_trace} while CodeLlama gains only +0.93pp (n.s.), suggesting loop utilization reflects architectural capacity to exploit structured iteration patterns rather than a universal signal.\nWithin Tier~2, the larger Qwen-7B outgains Qwen-1.5B (+10.92 vs.\\ +7.19pp, $p{<}.0001$), the only condition where scale provides a meaningful advantage.\n\n\\paragraph{Baselines.}\nThe strongest regex heuristic (exception class name matching) trails SFT \\cond{exception\\_only} by 6.22pp ($p{<}.001$); the gap concentrates on boundary cases where SFT distinguishes caught from uncaught exceptions, correctly resolving 6/8 ambiguous samples vs.\\ 0/8 for regex (Appendix~\\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).\nZero-shot prompting spans a 42pp range across models; SFT compresses this to 0.50pp with 4,720 training samples (Figure~\\ref{fig:zeroshot_vs_sft}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:zeroshot_cross_model}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{forest_plot_tier_boundary}\n\\caption{Cross-model accuracy with 95\\% Wilson CIs across all conditions and four models. The shaded region highlights the Tier~1/2 boundary gap ({>}12pp). Within Tier~1, all models converge to $\\geq$99.08\\%; within Tier~2, performance is model-dependent.}\n\\label{fig:forest_plot}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\\textwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{budget_curve}\n\\end{minipage}\\hfill\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\\textwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{token_distribution}\n\\end{minipage}\n\\caption{(\\textbf{Left}) Cross-model tail-truncation budget curve ($N{=}1{,}190$, four models). All models remain near baseline at tail-256/384, then jump sharply at 384$\\to$512 as the exception traceback is captured (shaded). Dashed: \\cond{exception\\_only} range; dotted: \\cond{no\\_trace} range. (\\textbf{Right}) Input token length distribution across trace conditions (training set, $N{=}4{,}720$). Dashed line: 2,048-token context window. \\cond{exception\\_only} inputs (median 150 tokens) fit entirely within the window, while \\cond{full\\_trace} inputs frequently exceed it, triggering truncation.}\n\\label{fig:trace_length}\n\\label{fig:token_distribution}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Format Marker Ablation}\n\\label{sec:nomarker}\n\nThe \\cond{exception\\_only} format includes a ``[No exception data in trace]'' marker for pass-class samples.\nRetraining with all format markers removed (\\cond{nomarker}) yields a modest drop (${\\sim}$2pp on both Qwen-7B and Qwen-1.5B), accounting for only ${\\sim}$9\\% of the total exception lift over \\cond{no\\_trace}.\nException events thus provide two signal layers: structural markers (${\\sim}$2pp, regex-learnable) and semantic content (${\\sim}$22pp, requiring SFT to distinguish caught from uncaught exceptions).\nBoth layers show no degradation at smaller scale.\n\n\\subsection{Information Density Decomposition}\n\\label{sec:density}\n\nThe gap between \\cond{full\\_trace} and VOTA decomposes via \\cond{full\\_trace\\_label\\_only}, which retains the full trace input but masks all trace tokens from the loss (Table~\\ref{tab:density}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Information density decomposition on Qwen-7B. Gradient masking (\\cond{label\\_only}) places an upper bound on backward-pass dilution; the remaining gap reflects inference-time input factors. 3-seed means.}\n\\label{tab:density}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ vs.\\ full\\_trace} & \\textbf{Gap explained} \\\\\n\\midrule\nfull\\_trace & 81.76 & --- & --- \\\\\nlabel\\_only & 85.94{\\scriptsize$\\pm$1.12} & +4.18pp & 24\\% \\\\\nVOTA & 99.05{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.38} & +17.29pp & 100\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\nGradient masking places an upper bound on backward-pass dilution: the +4.18pp gain bounds the backward-pass contribution to ${\\leq}$24\\% of the full gap.\nThe remaining majority of the gap persists even with identical loss computation, suggesting that interference from thousands of irrelevant trace lines at inference---rather than training signal dilution---is the dominant factor.\nCross-model evidence corroborates this: the tail-truncation phase transition at ${\\sim}$500 tokens (\\S\\ref{sec:trace_length}) coincides with exception traceback length, and cross-scale validation on Qwen-1.5B yields a consistent pattern (gradient masking explains a similar minority share).\nCodeLlama and DeepSeek exhibit label-only collapse, limiting the decomposition to models whose tokenizers preserve sufficient trace structure.\nError analysis confirms all \\cond{full\\_trace} errors occur where truncation removes diagnostic markers (Figure~\\ref{fig:token_distribution}).\nThe two factors may interact, and the decomposition is approximate (Qwen family, 2 scales, 3 seeds).\n\n\\subsection{Additional Analyses}\n\\label{sec:additional}\n\n\\paragraph{Tail-truncation budget curve.}\n\\label{sec:trace_length}\nA cross-model budget sweep reveals a sharp phase transition between 384 and 512 tail tokens (Figure~\\ref{fig:trace_length}).\nBelow this threshold, all models remain near baseline regardless of how many tokens are retained.\nAbove it, accuracy jumps by 13--22pp in a single step, converging into a narrow band by tail-1024.\nThe transition zone coincides with the typical exception traceback length (${\\sim}$400--500 tokens), providing spatial evidence that diagnostic events in the trace tail drive the accuracy gains.\n\nTail-truncation dramatically outperforms head-truncation: retaining the last $k$ tokens exceeds retaining the first $k$ by 15--23pp across models.\nUnder head-truncation, accuracy is non-monotonic and confined to a narrow band, because mid-length prefixes retain enough irrelevant content to mislead the model while discarding diagnostic events near the trace terminus (Appendix~\\ref{sec:trace_length_full}).\n\n\\paragraph{Calibration.}\n\\label{sec:calibration}\nCalibration mirrors the tier hierarchy: \\cond{exception\\_only} achieves ECE\\,=\\,0.003 vs.\\ 0.171 for \\cond{no\\_trace}, a 63$\\times$ gap (Figure~\\ref{fig:calibration}).\nThis advantage generalizes across all four models (ECE~${<}$~0.007; Appendix~\\ref{sec:calibration_full}).\n\n\\paragraph{Error orthogonality and exception types.}\n\\cond{exception\\_only}'s residual errors have zero overlap with all other conditions' error sets (Jaccard\\,=\\,0.000; Figure~\\ref{fig:error_overlap}), which confirms it operates on an independent signal dimension.\nAll standard exception types achieve 100\\% accuracy; the only failures arise from \\textit{GeneratorExit}, a benign \\textit{BaseException} subclass (Appendix~\\ref{sec:exception_type}).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-dataset generalization.}\n\\label{sec:debugbench}\nDebugBench \\citep{tian2024debugbench} ($N{=}426$) preserves the component ranking (\\cond{exception\\_only} 100\\%, VOTA 99.77\\%, \\cond{no\\_trace} 96.71\\%), though narrow margins reflect a ceiling effect from structurally distinctive mutations (Appendix~\\ref{sec:debugbench_full}).\n\n",
        "numLines": 128,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 128
      }
    },
```

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "653586bc-655c-4f15-908d-2ec5c50df8bd",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_4/projects/trace-verdict-study/docs/paper/method.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_4/projects/trace-verdict-study/docs/paper/method.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Method}\n\n\\subsection{Task Definition}\n\nWe study \\emph{verdict prediction}: given a Python function, a test input, and an optional execution trace, predict whether the test passes or fails.\nThe model produces a single token (\\textit{pass} or \\textit{fail}), making this a binary classification task.\nUnlike code generation \\citep{chen2025tracepile} or output prediction \\citep{gu2024cruxeval}, verdict prediction isolates the model's ability to detect faults from its ability to simulate execution.\nThe discrete binary outcome makes the effect of each trace component directly measurable, providing cleaner ablation contrasts than generation tasks where output quality varies continuously.\n\n\\subsection{Dataset}\n\\label{sec:dataset}\n\nWe sampled 10 candidate solutions per problem from Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct (temperature 0.8, top\\_p 0.95) for 591 problems from HumanEval \\citep{chen2021evaluating} (164) and MBPP-sanitized \\citep{austin2021program} (427), executed each against canonical tests with \\textit{sys.settrace} to obtain line-level traces and pass/fail verdicts, yielding 5,910 pairs (67\\% pass, 33\\% fail).\nThe dataset is split 80/20 by problem ID into 4,720 training and 1,190 test samples (majority baseline: 71.51\\%).\nThe \\cond{exception\\_only} format collapses passing solutions into identical rows (39.45\\% train, 48.99\\% test duplicates); these duplicates arise from multiple candidate solutions per problem, with the train--test split enforced at the problem level (472 train / 119 test problems, zero crossover; Appendix~\\ref{sec:dedup_stats}).\n\n\\subsection{Trace Representations}\n\nWe define seven conditions that vary the trace information available.\n\\cond{no\\_trace} provides only source code and test input.\n\\cond{full\\_trace} includes the complete line-by-line trace with variable states.\n\\cond{VOTA} combines exception events, assertion-point comparisons, and loop compression (Appendix~\\ref{sec:vota_algorithm}).\nThe remaining conditions isolate individual VOTA components:\n\\cond{exception\\_only} retains only exception and error events;\n\\cond{ae\\_only} retains only assertion-point actual/expected pairs;\n\\cond{loop\\_only} retains only loop compression summaries.\n\\cond{VOTA\\_no\\_ae} is VOTA without assertion-point information.\n\n\\subsection{Models and Training}\n\nWe evaluate four models spanning three architecture families: \\textbf{Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B/1.5B-Instruct} \\citep{qwen2025coder}, \\textbf{CodeLlama-13B-Instruct} \\citep{roziere2024code}, and \\textbf{DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct} \\citep{deepseek2024coder}, covering a 1.5B--13B parameter range (8.7$\\times$).\nAll conditions use LoRA \\citep{hu2022lora} (rank=16, $\\alpha$=32, dropout=0.05) on all linear layers, 3 epochs, effective batch size 32, LR $2 \\times 10^{-4}$ with linear warmup/decay, max sequence length 2{,}048 tokens.\nSequences exceeding this limit are truncated while preserving the verdict label.\nThe primary seed is 42; seeds 123 and 456 test \\cond{exception\\_only} robustness (Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_details}).\n\n\\subsection{Loss Ablation}\n\nTo disentangle training signal from input signal quality, we introduce \\cond{full\\_trace\\_label\\_only}: identical full-trace input but loss computed only on the verdict token (trace positions masked).\nImprovement over standard \\cond{full\\_trace} is attributable to backward-pass signal dilution; the remaining gap to VOTA reflects inference-time input complexity.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Component ablation on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$). Tier~1 signals achieve near-perfect accuracy; combining them (VOTA) slightly dilutes performance. Entries marked $^\\dag$/$^\\S$/$^\\ddag$ are 3-seed mean$\\pm$std; per-seed breakdowns in Appendix~\\ref{sec:full_results}. Seed $\\sigma$ negatively correlates with density (0.00 for exception to 0.52 for loop).}\n\\label{tab:ablation}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Components} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{Tier} \\\\\n\\midrule\nae\\_only & assertion-point & 99.64$^\\dag$ & [99.26, 99.87] & 1 \\\\\nexception\\_only & exception events & \\textbf{99.83} & [99.58, 100.00] & 1 \\\\\nVOTA & exc+ae+loop & 99.05$^\\S$ & [98.49, 99.50] & --- \\\\\nVOTA\\_no\\_ae & exc+loop & 99.41 & [98.90, 99.80] & --- \\\\\nloop\\_only & loop compression & 86.05$^\\ddag$ & [83.97, 87.96] & 2 \\\\\nfull\\_trace & complete trace & 81.76 & [79.70, 83.90] & --- \\\\\nno\\_trace & none & 75.13 & [72.77, 77.48] & --- \\\\\n\\midrule\nRegex (best) & exc.\\ type regex & 93.61 & --- & --- \\\\\n3-shot (Qwen-7B) & in-context learning & 90.84 & --- & --- \\\\\nZero-shot (4 models) & prompting & 51.0--93.5 & --- & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n",
        "numLines": 66,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 66
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/method.tex"
```

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "cd829f6b-8073-4c9b-aa84-52cb15e178c8",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_4/projects/trace-verdict-study/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_4/projects/trace-verdict-study/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\subsection{Execution Traces for Code LLMs}\n\nRecent work has scaled trace-augmented training: TracePile \\citep{chen2025tracepile} pre-trained on 2.6M execution-annotated samples, while \\citet{liu2023codeexecution} pre-trained models to predict execution outputs from traces.\n\\citet{wang2025dcsh} found trace utility to be task- and format-dependent but did not decompose individual trace components; relative to their work, we contribute a two-tier signal hierarchy, a phase transition in trace-length budgets, calibration analysis, and cross-architecture validation.\nNExT \\citep{ni2024next} and ET-CoT \\citep{eguchi2025etcot} leveraged trace chain-of-thought for execution reasoning, and \\citet{ferrando2025execute} found compact traces comparable to full traces, aligning with our density finding.\nCRUXEval \\citep{gu2024cruxeval}, extended to 19 languages by CRUXEval-X \\citep{xu2025cruxevalx}, established execution reasoning benchmarks, though neither decomposes which trace components contribute to \\emph{classification} accuracy.\n\\citet{shi2023distracted} showed LLMs are easily distracted by irrelevant context, providing a complementary explanation for why verbose traces impair SFT learning.\nIn the prompt compression literature, LLMLingua \\citep{jiang2023llmlingua} and Selective Context \\citep{li2023selectivecontext} showed that pruning low-information tokens preserves downstream accuracy; TraceCoder \\citep{huang2026tracecoder} and InspectCoder \\citep{wang2025inspectcoder} apply selective trace extraction for automated debugging; and \\citet{haque2025traces} found raw traces provide limited repair benefit.\nOur density decomposition complements these methods by quantifying \\emph{why} compression helps (gradient dilution explains ${\\leq}$24\\% of the gap; the remainder reflects inference-time interference).\n\n\\subsection{Verdict Prediction and Fault Detection}\n\nVerdict prediction is an instance of the longstanding test oracle problem \\citep{barr2015oracle}.\nTRACED \\citep{ding2024traced} showed execution-aware pretraining improves vulnerability detection; DeepDFA \\citep{steenhoek2024deepdfa} and learned program slicing \\citep{yadavally2024slicing} combined classical program analysis with deep learning for defect detection.\nIn patch correctness prediction, representation learning of code changes has progressed from CC2Vec \\citep{hoang2020cc2vec} to LLM4PatchCorrect \\citep{zhou2024llm4patch} (84.4\\%) and APPT \\citep{appt2024} (79.7\\%); \\citet{tian2020patchcorrectness} evaluated change representations for predicting repair correctness.\nFuseFL \\citep{widyasari2024fusefl} and \\citet{foster2025mutation} demonstrated that structured fault signals (stack traces, mutation outcomes) improve localization; the equivalent-mutant problem \\citep{papadakis2019mutation} poses an analogous classification challenge.\nSpectrum-based fault localization (SBFL) ranks program elements by suspiciousness from test spectra \\citep{abreu2007accuracy,jones2002visualization,wong2014dstar}; recent LLM-based approaches such as AutoFL \\citep{kang2024autofl} and AgentFL \\citep{qin2024agentfl} share our insight that anomalous execution events carry the strongest diagnostic signal.\nIn agent-based repair, RepairAgent \\citep{bouzenia2025repairagent}, Agentless \\citep{xia2024agentless}, SWE-agent \\citep{yang2024sweagent}, and OpenHands \\citep{wang2025openhands} all incorporate execution feedback in repair workflows; in the SWE-bench ecosystem \\citep{jimenez2024swebench}, these agents rely on test verdict signals to guide patch selection, yet which trace components carry that signal most efficiently remains unstudied.\nLDB \\citep{zhong2024ldb} applies segment-level trace verification for debugging, and SelfDebug \\citep{chen2024selfdebug} and Reflexion \\citep{shinn2023reflexion} iteratively refine code using execution outcomes.\nNone of these works isolate individual trace components or compare against rule-based baselines.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-model verdict accuracy (\\%, $N{=}1{,}190$) across four models (1.5B--13B) and three architecture families. Multi-seed entries report mean$\\pm$std over 2--3 seeds. Bold: best per model. Full per-seed breakdowns in Appendix~\\ref{sec:full_results}; per-class metrics in Appendix~\\ref{sec:per_class}.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Qwen-7B} & \\textbf{Qwen-1.5B} & \\textbf{CL-13B} & \\textbf{DS-6.7B} \\\\\n\\midrule\nexception\\_only & \\textbf{99.83}{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.00} & \\textbf{99.83}{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.00} & \\textbf{99.89}{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.10} & \\textbf{99.39}{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.10} \\\\\nae\\_only & 99.64{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.49} & 98.83{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.36} & 99.08{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.00} & 98.53{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.30} \\\\\nVOTA & 99.05{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.38} & 99.54{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.06} & 98.74{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.24} & 99.25$^\\ddag$ \\\\\nloop\\_only & 86.05{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.52} & 79.54{\\scriptsize$\\pm$1.42} & 77.40{\\scriptsize$\\pm$1.54} & 80.31{\\scriptsize$\\pm$1.65} \\\\\nno\\_trace & 75.13 & 72.35{\\scriptsize$\\pm$4.48} & 76.47 & 73.36 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMajority baseline & \\multicolumn{4}{c}{71.51} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\par\\raggedright\\footnotesize\nDeepSeek \\cond{exception\\_only} 3 seeds: 99.33/99.50/99.33 ($\\sigma{=}$0.10pp).\nVOTA on Qwen-7B: 99.41/98.66/99.08 ($\\sigma{=}$0.38).\n\\cond{ae\\_only} 3 seeds: 99.92/99.08/99.92 (mean 99.64\\%); Qwen-7B \\cond{loop\\_only} 3 seeds: 85.63/85.88/86.64 (mean 86.05\\%).\nQwen-1.5B \\cond{exception\\_only}: 99.83\\% across 3 seeds ($\\sigma{=}$0).\n$^\\ddag$DeepSeek VOTA 3 seeds: 84.45/99.16/99.33; seed 42 (84.45\\%, fail recall\\,=\\,45.4\\%) is a training failure due to tokenizer-driven truncation (Appendix~\\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}), reported as 2-seed mean.\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Calibration and Training Dynamics}\n\nNeural network calibration \\citep{guo2017calibration}, extended to LLMs \\citep{kadavath2022language,geng2024calibration}, studies confidence--correctness alignment; \\citet{spiess2025calibration} specifically addressed calibration for code models.\nOur ECE ${<}$ 0.007 across four models suggests compact inputs improve calibration beyond accuracy gains alone.\nJust-in-time defect prediction \\citep{kamei2013large,hoang2019deepjit,pornprasit2021jitline} shares our fault detection motivation but operates on code change features rather than execution traces; our work complements this line by showing that execution-time fault signals can be classified with near-perfect accuracy when properly extracted.\nCurriculum learning \\citep{bengio2009curriculum}, dataset cartography via training dynamics \\citep{swayamdipta2020cartography}, and data selection methods \\citep{sachdeva2024dataefficient,xie2023doremi} demonstrate that data quality and ordering improve convergence---consistent with our finding that compact exceptions enable convergence within 50 steps while verbose traces trap the model in oscillation across all 444 training steps.\n\\citet{yang2024generalization} further showed that fine-tuned LLMs generalize better on concise inputs, providing a complementary perspective on why information density governs SFT effectiveness.\n\n",
        "numLines": 61,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 61
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/related_work.tex"
  },
  "type": "attachment",
```

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "9ef0d993-7267-4928-8153-0f7d27d183ae",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_4/projects/trace-verdict-study/docs/paper/discussion.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_4/projects/trace-verdict-study/docs/paper/discussion.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig2_calibration}\n\\caption{Reliability diagrams for four trace conditions on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$). \\cond{exception\\_only} (ECE=0.003) clusters near the diagonal; \\cond{no\\_trace} (ECE=0.171) shows systematic overconfidence.}\n\\label{fig:calibration}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe central finding is that information density governs learning efficiency for trace-based verdict prediction.\nException events concentrate the fault signal in a compact format (median 150 tokens), while non-exception components record \\emph{how} the program ran, not \\emph{whether} it failed.\nThis explains three patterns: (1)~adding information beyond exceptions provides no benefit---indeed, VOTA underperforms \\cond{exception\\_only} (Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}); (2)~the tail-truncation phase transition at ${\\sim}$500 tokens coincides with the diagnostic-event boundary (Figure~\\ref{fig:trace_length}); and (3)~the SFT advantage over regex concentrates on caught-exception boundary cases (\\S\\ref{sec:nomarker}).\nThe narrow cross-model gap (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}) supports density, not capacity, as the binding constraint.\n\n\\paragraph{SFT vs.\\ regex: semantic understanding.}\nFormat-marker removal drops accuracy by only ${\\sim}$2pp, leaving a clear gap over the best regex.\nOn boundary samples with caught exceptions, SFT correctly classifies 6/8 vs.\\ 0/8 for regex ($p{=}.041$), confirming the signal is semantic rather than syntactic.\nThis distinction matters for deployment: regex baselines can detect most exceptions through surface-level pattern matching, but fail precisely on the ambiguous cases (caught exceptions within \\textit{try/except} blocks) where correct classification requires understanding the exception's role in program control flow.\nSFT learns this distinction from fewer than 5{,}000 training examples, suggesting that the relevant semantic features are readily extractable from pretrained code representations when the input is sufficiently compact.\n\n\\paragraph{Near-perfect floor.}\nThe two residual errors (both \\textit{GeneratorExit}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:error_overlap}) are shared across configurations and models.\nOne seed achieves 100\\%, confirming 99.83\\% as a convergence \\emph{floor}, not a ceiling.\nThe error overlap analysis (Figure~\\ref{fig:error_overlap}) reveals that these errors are orthogonal to all other conditions' error sets (Jaccard = 0.000), confirming that \\cond{exception\\_only} operates on an independent signal dimension.\nThe practical implication is that the two residual errors cannot be resolved by adding more trace information; they require disambiguating benign \\textit{BaseException} subclasses from genuine faults, a representation-level fix rather than a data quantity issue.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{error_overlap}\n\\caption{Pairwise Jaccard similarity between error sets (Qwen-7B, $N{=}1{,}190$). \\cond{exception\\_only} has zero overlap with all other conditions, confirming it operates on an independent signal dimension.}\n\\label{fig:error_overlap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Tier hierarchy and design principles.}\nThe two-tier structure (Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot}) suggests a hierarchical extraction strategy: extract exception events first, resort to Tier~2 signals only when no exception is raised.\nWithin Tier~1, both \\cond{exception\\_only} and \\cond{ae\\_only} achieve near-perfect accuracy independently, yet combining them in VOTA slightly \\emph{degrades} performance---a counter-intuitive finding that underscores how additional signal can introduce noise when the primary signal is already sufficient.\nLoop benefit is model-dependent (Qwen-7B gains +10.92pp while CodeLlama gains only +0.93pp), confirming it as an architecture-level capacity to exploit structured iteration patterns rather than a universal signal.\nThis density--efficiency link should transfer to any trace-based task where compact diagnostic events exist within verbose runtime logs---for example, crash triage in continuous integration, where stack traces carry far more signal than full execution histories.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model robustness and scale independence.}\nThe cross-model analysis reveals that \\cond{exception\\_only}'s accuracy range is ${<}$1pp across four models (1.5B--13B) vs.\\ ${\\sim}$9pp for \\cond{loop\\_only}, demonstrating that compact inputs eliminate the architecture-dependent variance that plagues verbose representations.\nThis scale independence is striking: the smallest model (Qwen-1.5B at 1.5B parameters) matches the largest (CodeLlama-13B at 13B parameters) when given the same compact input, whereas \\cond{no\\_trace} accuracy scales monotonically with model capacity (Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_scale}).\nThe zero-shot comparison reinforces this: SFT compresses a 42.44pp cross-architecture range to just 0.50pp with only 4{,}720 training samples (Figure~\\ref{fig:zeroshot_vs_sft}), making exception-based verdict prediction accessible to smaller models without sacrificing accuracy.\nLabel-only loss on high-truncation tokenizers causes model collapse, further confirming density as the binding constraint: when the tokenizer is inefficient, even masking trace tokens from the loss cannot compensate for the inference-time interference from thousands of irrelevant tokens.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{zeroshot_vs_sft}\n\\caption{Zero-shot vs.\\ SFT \\cond{exception\\_only} accuracy across four models ($N{=}1{,}190$). SFT compresses the cross-architecture variance from 42.44pp to 0.50pp.}\n\\label{fig:zeroshot_vs_sft}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Exception events capture test verdicts, not just crashes.}\nA natural concern is that exception-based classification merely detects program crashes.\nIn fact, 55.5\\% of failures in our dataset are silent logic errors---the program terminates normally but produces incorrect output.\n\\cond{exception\\_only} nonetheless achieves 100\\% fail recall because test assertions generate \\textit{AssertionError} events that explicitly mark the discrepancy between actual and expected behavior.\nThis means the ``exception'' signal subsumes both crash-type and assertion-type failures, making it a complete verdict signal rather than a crash detector.\n\n\\paragraph{Alternative explanations.}\nThree alternatives merit discussion.\n\\emph{Length asymmetry}: padding all inputs to the maximum sequence length leaves accuracy unchanged, ruling out a confound from batch-level length variation.\n\\emph{Format-marker familiarity}: removing markers accounts for only ${\\sim}$9\\% of the exception lift, though familiarity with exception formats in pretraining data cannot be fully controlled for.\n\\emph{Positional concentration}: diagnostic information concentrates in the trace tail (\\S\\ref{sec:trace_length}); the tail-truncation budget curve localizes the critical threshold to ${\\sim}$400--500 tokens, consistent with typical exception traceback length, but fully disentangling positional proximity from semantic density requires future work with controlled position-shuffled experiments.\n\n\\paragraph{Connections to prompt compression.}\nOur findings align with the prompt compression literature: LLMLingua \\citep{jiang2023llmlingua} and Selective Context \\citep{li2023selectivecontext} showed that pruning low-information tokens preserves downstream accuracy.\nThe trace domain provides a natural instance of this phenomenon---exception events are effectively a domain-specific compression that retains the diagnostic tokens and discards the execution log.\nThe information density decomposition (\\S\\ref{sec:density}) quantifies this: removing all non-diagnostic tokens accounts for the majority of the performance gap, while backward-pass gradient dilution explains at most 24\\%.\n\n\\paragraph{Learning dynamics.}\n\\cond{exception\\_only} converges within 50 steps and saturates by step 150, while \\cond{full\\_trace} oscillates across all 444 training steps without reaching stable convergence (Figure~\\ref{fig:training_dynamics}).\nSeed variance negatively correlates with input density (Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}): \\cond{exception\\_only} shows zero cross-seed variance ($\\sigma{=}$0.00) while \\cond{loop\\_only} exhibits the highest ($\\sigma{=}$0.52).\nThis pattern suggests that compact inputs not only improve final accuracy but also stabilize the optimization landscape, reducing sensitivity to random initialization.\nFrom a practical standpoint, this means exception-based models can be trained with a single seed and minimal hyperparameter tuning, whereas verbose-trace models require multiple seeds and careful early-stopping selection.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{training_dynamics}\n\\caption{Training dynamics comparison. \\cond{exception\\_only} (blue) converges within 50 steps and saturates by step 150. \\cond{full\\_trace} with label-only loss (red, dashed) oscillates without stable convergence across all 444 training steps.}\n\\label{fig:training_dynamics}\n\\end{figure}\n",
```

```json
    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",
```

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	
3	\subsection{Execution Traces for Code LLMs}
4	
5	Recent work has scaled trace-augmented training: TracePile \citep{chen2025tracepile} pre-trained on 2.6M execution-annotated samples, while \citet{liu2023codeexecution} pre-trained models to predict execution outputs from traces.
6	\citet{wang2025dcsh} found trace utility to be task- and format-dependent but did not decompose individual trace components; relative to their work, we contribute a two-tier signal hierarchy, a phase transition in trace-length budgets, calibration analysis, and cross-architecture validation.
7	NExT \citep{ni2024next} and ET-CoT \citep{eguchi2025etcot} leveraged trace chain-of-thought for execution reasoning, and \citet{ferrando2025execute} found compact traces comparable to full traces, aligning with our density finding.
8	CRUXEval \citep{gu2024cruxeval}, extended to 19 languages by CRUXEval-X \citep{xu2025cruxevalx}, established execution reasoning benchmarks, though neither decomposes which trace components contribute to \emph{classification} accuracy.
9	\citet{shi2023distracted} showed LLMs are easily distracted by irrelevant context, providing a complementary explanation for why verbose traces impair SFT learning.
10	In the prompt compression literature, LLMLingua \citep{jiang2023llmlingua} and Selective Context \citep{li2023selectivecontext} showed that pruning low-information tokens preserves downstream accuracy; TraceCoder \citep{huang2026tracecoder} and InspectCoder \citep{wang2025inspectcoder} apply selective trace extraction for automated debugging; and \citet{haque2025traces} found raw traces provide limited repair benefit.
11	Our density decomposition complements these methods by quantifying \emph{why} compression helps (gradient dilution explains ${\leq}$24\% of the gap; the remainder reflects inference-time interference).
12	
13	\subsection{Verdict Prediction and Fault Detection}
14	
15	Verdict prediction is an instance of the longstanding test oracle problem \citep{barr2015oracle}.
16	TRACED \citep{ding2024traced} showed execution-aware pretraining improves vulnerability detection; DeepDFA \citep{steenhoek2024deepdfa} and learned program slicing \citep{yadavally2024slicing} combined classical program analysis with deep learning for defect detection.
17	In patch correctness prediction, representation learning of code changes has progressed from CC2Vec \citep{hoang2020cc2vec} to LLM4PatchCorrect \citep{zhou2024llm4patch} (84.4\%) and APPT \citep{appt2024} (79.7\%); \citet{tian2020patchcorrectness} evaluated change representations for predicting repair correctness.
18	FuseFL \citep{widyasari2024fusefl} and \citet{foster2025mutation} demonstrated that structured fault signals (stack traces, mutation outcomes) improve localization; the equivalent-mutant problem \citep{papadakis2019mutation} poses an analogous classification challenge.
19	Spectrum-based fault localization (SBFL) ranks program elements by suspiciousness from test spectra \citep{abreu2007accuracy,jones2002visualization,wong2014dstar}; recent LLM-based approaches such as AutoFL \citep{kang2024autofl} and AgentFL \citep{qin2024agentfl} share our insight that anomalous execution events carry the strongest diagnostic signal.
20	In agent-based repair, RepairAgent \citep{bouzenia2025repairagent}, Agentless \citep{xia2024agentless}, SWE-agent \citep{yang2024sweagent}, and OpenHands \citep{wang2025openhands} all incorporate execution feedback in repair workflows; in the SWE-bench ecosystem \citep{jimenez2024swebench}, these agents rely on test verdict signals to guide patch selection, yet which trace components carry that signal most efficiently remains unstudied.
21	LDB \citep{zhong2024ldb} applies segment-level trace verification for debugging, and SelfDebug \citep{chen2024selfdebug} and Reflexion \citep{shinn2023reflexion} iteratively refine code using execution outcomes.
22	None of these works isolate individual trace components or compare against rule-based baselines.
23	
24	\begin{table}[t]
25	\centering
26	\caption{Cross-model verdict accuracy (\%, $N{=}1{,}190$) across four models (1.5B--13B) and three architecture families. Multi-seed entries report mean$\pm$std over 2--3 seeds. Bold: best per model. Full per-seed breakdowns in Appendix~\ref{sec:full_results}; per-class metrics in Appendix~\ref{sec:per_class}.}
27	\label{tab:cross_model}
28	\small

52	
53	\subsection{Calibration and Training Dynamics}
54	
55	Neural network calibration \citep{guo2017calibration}, extended to LLMs \citep{kadavath2022language,geng2024calibration}, studies confidence--correctness alignment; \citet{spiess2025calibration} specifically addressed calibration for code models.
56	Our ECE ${<}$ 0.007 across four models suggests compact inputs improve calibration beyond accuracy gains alone.
57	Just-in-time defect prediction \citep{kamei2013large,hoang2019deepjit,pornprasit2021jitline} shares our fault detection motivation but operates on code change features rather than execution traces; our work complements this line by showing that execution-time fault signals can be classified with near-perfect accuracy when properly extracted.
58	Curriculum learning \citep{bengio2009curriculum}, dataset cartography via training dynamics \citep{swayamdipta2020cartography}, and data selection methods \citep{sachdeva2024dataefficient,xie2023doremi} demonstrate that data quality and ordering improve convergence---consistent with our finding that compact exceptions enable convergence within 50 steps while verbose traces trap the model in oscillation across all 444 training steps.
59	\citet{yang2024generalization} further showed that fine-tuned LLMs generalize better on concise inputs, providing a complementary perspective on why information density governs SFT effectiveness.
60
```

```json
      "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\subsection{Execution Traces for Code LLMs}\n\nRecent work has scaled trace-augmented training: TracePile \\citep{chen2025tracepile} pre-trained on 2.6M execution-annotated samples, while \\citet{liu2023codeexecution} pre-trained models to predict execution outputs from traces.\n\\citet{wang2025dcsh} found trace utility to be task- and format-dependent but did not decompose individual trace components; relative to their work, we contribute a two-tier signal hierarchy, a phase transition in trace-length budgets, calibration analysis, and cross-architecture validation.\nNExT \\citep{ni2024next} and ET-CoT \\citep{eguchi2025etcot} leveraged trace chain-of-thought for execution reasoning, and \\citet{ferrando2025execute} found compact traces comparable to full traces, aligning with our density finding.\nCRUXEval \\citep{gu2024cruxeval}, extended to 19 languages by CRUXEval-X \\citep{xu2025cruxevalx}, established execution reasoning benchmarks, though neither decomposes which trace components contribute to \\emph{classification} accuracy.\n\\citet{shi2023distracted} showed LLMs are easily distracted by irrelevant context, providing a complementary explanation for why verbose traces impair SFT learning.\nIn the prompt compression literature, LLMLingua \\citep{jiang2023llmlingua} and Selective Context \\citep{li2023selectivecontext} showed that pruning low-information tokens preserves downstream accuracy; TraceCoder \\citep{huang2026tracecoder} and InspectCoder \\citep{wang2025inspectcoder} apply selective trace extraction for automated debugging; and \\citet{haque2025traces} found raw traces provide limited repair benefit.\nOur density decomposition complements these methods by quantifying \\emph{why} compression helps (gradient dilution explains ${\\leq}$24\\% of the gap; the remainder reflects inference-time interference).\n\n\\subsection{Verdict Prediction and Fault Detection}\n\nVerdict prediction is an instance of the longstanding test oracle problem \\citep{barr2015oracle}.\nTRACED \\citep{ding2024traced} showed execution-aware pretraining improves vulnerability detection; DeepDFA \\citep{steenhoek2024deepdfa} and learned program slicing \\citep{yadavally2024slicing} combined classical program analysis with deep learning for defect detection.\nIn patch correctness prediction, representation learning of code changes has progressed from CC2Vec \\citep{hoang2020cc2vec} to LLM4PatchCorrect \\citep{zhou2024llm4patch} (84.4\\%) and APPT \\citep{appt2024} (79.7\\%); \\citet{tian2020patchcorrectness} evaluated change representations for predicting repair correctness.\nFuseFL \\citep{widyasari2024fusefl} and \\citet{foster2025mutation} demonstrated that structured fault signals (stack traces, mutation outcomes) improve localization; the equivalent-mutant problem \\citep{papadakis2019mutation} poses an analogous classification challenge.\nSpectrum-based fault localization (SBFL) ranks program elements by suspiciousness from test spectra \\citep{abreu2007accuracy,jones2002visualization,wong2014dstar}; recent LLM-based approaches such as AutoFL \\citep{kang2024autofl} and AgentFL \\citep{qin2024agentfl} share our insight that anomalous execution events carry the strongest diagnostic signal.\nIn agent-based repair, RepairAgent \\citep{bouzenia2025repairagent}, Agentless \\citep{xia2024agentless}, SWE-agent \\citep{yang2024sweagent}, and OpenHands \\citep{wang2025openhands} all incorporate execution feedback in repair workflows; in the SWE-bench ecosystem \\citep{jimenez2024swebench}, these agents rely on test verdict signals to guide patch selection, yet which trace components carry that signal most efficiently remains unstudied.\nLDB \\citep{zhong2024ldb} applies segment-level trace verification for debugging, and SelfDebug \\citep{chen2024selfdebug} and Reflexion \\citep{shinn2023reflexion} iteratively refine code using execution outcomes.\nNone of these works isolate individual trace components or compare against rule-based baselines.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-model verdict accuracy (\\%, $N{=}1{,}190$) across four models (1.5B--13B) and three architecture families. Multi-seed entries report mean$\\pm$std over 2--3 seeds. Bold: best per model. Full per-seed breakdowns in Appendix~\\ref{sec:full_results}; per-class metrics in Appendix~\\ref{sec:per_class}.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Qwen-7B} & \\textbf{Qwen-1.5B} & \\textbf{CL-13B} & \\textbf{DS-6.7B} \\\\\n\\midrule\nexception\\_only & \\textbf{99.83}{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.00} & \\textbf{99.83}{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.00} & \\textbf{99.89}{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.10} & \\textbf{99.39}{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.10} \\\\\nae\\_only & 99.64{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.49} & 98.83{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.36} & 99.08{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.00} & 98.53{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.30} \\\\\nVOTA & 99.05{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.38} & 99.54{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.06} & 98.74{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.24} & 99.25$^\\ddag$ \\\\\nloop\\_only & 86.05{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.52} & 79.54{\\scriptsize$\\pm$1.42} & 77.40{\\scriptsize$\\pm$1.54} & 80.31{\\scriptsize$\\pm$1.65} \\\\\nno\\_trace & 75.13 & 72.35{\\scriptsize$\\pm$4.48} & 76.47 & 73.36 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMajority baseline & \\multicolumn{4}{c}{71.51} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\par\\raggedright\\footnotesize\nDeepSeek \\cond{exception\\_only} 3 seeds: 99.33/99.50/99.33 ($\\sigma{=}$0.10pp).\nVOTA on Qwen-7B: 99.41/98.66/99.08 ($\\sigma{=}$0.38).\n\\cond{ae\\_only} 3 seeds: 99.92/99.08/99.92 (mean 99.64\\%); Qwen-7B \\cond{loop\\_only} 3 seeds: 85.63/85.88/86.64 (mean 86.05\\%).\nQwen-1.5B \\cond{exception\\_only}: 99.83\\% across 3 seeds ($\\sigma{=}$0).\n$^\\ddag$DeepSeek VOTA 3 seeds: 84.45/99.16/99.33; seed 42 (84.45\\%, fail recall\\,=\\,45.4\\%) is a training failure due to tokenizer-driven truncation (Appendix~\\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}), reported as 2-seed mean.\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Calibration and Training Dynamics}\n\nNeural network calibration \\citep{guo2017calibration}, extended to LLMs \\citep{kadavath2022language,geng2024calibration}, studies confidence--correctness alignment; \\citet{spiess2025calibration} specifically addressed calibration for code models.\nOur ECE ${<}$ 0.007 across four models suggests compact inputs improve calibration beyond accuracy gains alone.\nJust-in-time defect prediction \\citep{kamei2013large,hoang2019deepjit,pornprasit2021jitline} shares our fault detection motivation but operates on code change features rather than execution traces; our work complements this line by showing that execution-time fault signals can be classified with near-perfect accuracy when properly extracted.\nCurriculum learning \\citep{bengio2009curriculum}, dataset cartography via training dynamics \\citep{swayamdipta2020cartography}, and data selection methods \\citep{sachdeva2024dataefficient,xie2023doremi} demonstrate that data quality and ordering improve convergence---consistent with our finding that compact exceptions enable convergence within 50 steps while verbose traces trap the model in oscillation across all 444 training steps.\n\\citet{yang2024generalization} further showed that fine-tuned LLMs generalize better on concise inputs, providing a complementary perspective on why information density governs SFT effectiveness.\n\n",
```

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Execution traces record a program's complete runtime behavior: every function call, variable assignment, and control flow decision.
4	Recent work has incorporated them into code language models for tasks from bug localization to program repair \citep{chen2025tracepile,ding2024traced,wang2025dcsh,ni2024next}, under the working assumption that richer runtime information yields better predictions.
5	\emph{We find the opposite.}
6	For verdict prediction (classifying whether a test passes or fails), feeding a model the complete execution trace produces accuracies of 72--82\%, barely above a source-code-only baseline, while a single compact component of the same trace reaches ${\geq}$99.33\% across all models tested.
7	Verbose traces do not just fail to help; they actively impair learning.
8	
9	Verdict prediction offers a clean testbed for isolating this effect.
10	As binary classification, it separates signal extraction from generation noise; as fault detection, the model need only detect failure, not simulate execution.
11	If the fault signal concentrates in a small trace subset, information density should matter more than quantity.
12	Prior work found no single trace representation consistently outperforms others \citep{wang2025dcsh}, but focused on generation tasks where this density hypothesis could not be cleanly tested.
13	
14	We conduct the first systematic component ablation of trace representations, decomposing VOTA (exception events + assertion comparisons + loop compression) into individual components across four models spanning 1.5B to 13B parameters and three architecture families \citep{qwen2025coder,roziere2024code,deepseek2024coder}, on 591 problems from HumanEval \citep{chen2021evaluating} and MBPP-sanitized \citep{austin2021program}.
15	The results are unambiguous: exception events (a median of 150 tokens encoding error type, stack frame, and message) reach ${\geq}$99.33\% accuracy across all models and seeds, converging within 50 training steps.
16	Full traces oscillate between 82--89\% without stable convergence even under controlled gradient masking. This confirms that information density, not model capacity, is the binding constraint (Figure~\ref{fig:training_dynamics}).
17	
18	This finding is robust to several potential confounds.
19	Removing format markers preserves accuracy within 2pp, and on 8 boundary samples where exceptions appear in passing code, the fine-tuned model correctly classifies 6/8 vs.\ 0/8 for a regex baseline, which confirms the signal is semantic (\S\ref{sec:nomarker}).
20	Gradient masking bounds the backward-pass contribution to ${\leq}$4pp (\S\ref{sec:density}), a tail-truncation budget sweep across four models reveals a phase transition at ${\sim}$500 tokens (\S\ref{sec:trace_length}), and cross-dataset transfer to DebugBench ($N{=}426$) preserves the component ranking (\S\ref{sec:debugbench}).
21	
22	Our contributions:
23	\begin{enumerate}
24	\item \textbf{Information density as the binding constraint on SFT learning}: training dynamics, gradient masking (${\leq}$4pp backward-pass contribution), and cross-model convergence confirm that compact trace inputs learn faster and more stably than verbose ones, independent of model scale (\S\ref{sec:ablation}, \S\ref{sec:density}).
25	\item \textbf{Exception events as the minimal sufficient signal}: ${\geq}$99.33\% across all four models with zero cross-seed variance on Qwen and sub-1pp cross-model range (\S\ref{sec:cross_model}).
26	\item \textbf{A two-tier diagnostic hierarchy with quantitative decomposition}: Tier~1 signals (exceptions) generalize across architectures ($<$1pp range), while Tier~2 (loops) is model-dependent (${\sim}$9pp range); a phase transition at ${\sim}$500 tail tokens localizes the diagnostic boundary (\S\ref{sec:ablation}, \S\ref{sec:trace_length}).
27	\end{enumerate}
28	
29	\begin{figure}[t]
30	\centering
31	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig3_cross_model_gap}
32	\caption{Verdict accuracy vs.\ model scale. \cond{exception\_only} maintains ${\geq}$99.33\% regardless of scale, while \cond{no\_trace} shows monotonic sensitivity. \cond{nomarker} (markers removed) shows no scale degradation.}
33	\label{fig:cross_scale}
34	\end{figure}
35	
```
