```text
1	\section{Method}
2	
3	\subsection{Task Definition}
4	
5	We study \emph{verdict prediction}: given a Python function, a test input, and an optional execution trace, predict whether the test passes or fails.
6	The model produces a single token (\textit{pass} or \textit{fail}), making this a binary classification task.
7	Unlike code generation \citep{chen2025tracepile} or output prediction \citep{gu2024cruxeval}, verdict prediction isolates the model's ability to detect faults from its ability to simulate execution.
8	The discrete binary outcome makes the effect of each trace component directly measurable, providing cleaner ablation contrasts than generation tasks where output quality varies continuously.
9	
10	\subsection{Dataset}
11	\label{sec:dataset}
12	
13	We sampled 10 candidate solutions per problem from Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct (temperature 0.8, top\_p 0.95) for 591 problems from HumanEval \citep{chen2021evaluating} (164) and MBPP-sanitized \citep{austin2021program} (427), executed each against canonical tests with \textit{sys.settrace} to obtain line-level traces and pass/fail verdicts, yielding 5,910 pairs (67\% pass, 33\% fail).
14	The dataset is split 80/20 by problem ID into 4,720 training and 1,190 test samples (majority baseline: 71.51\%).
15	The \cond{exception\_only} format collapses passing solutions into identical rows (39.45\% train, 48.99\% test duplicates); these duplicates arise from multiple candidate solutions per problem, with the train--test split enforced at the problem level (472 train / 119 test problems, zero crossover; Appendix~\ref{sec:dedup_stats}).
16	
17	\subsection{Trace Representations}
18	
19	We define seven conditions that vary the trace information available.
20	\cond{no\_trace} provides only source code and test input.
21	\cond{full\_trace} includes the complete line-by-line trace with variable states.
22	\cond{VOTA} combines exception events, assertion-point comparisons, and loop compression (Appendix~\ref{sec:vota_algorithm}).
23	The remaining conditions isolate individual VOTA components:
24	\cond{exception\_only} retains only exception and error events;
25	\cond{ae\_only} retains only assertion-point actual/expected pairs;
26	\cond{loop\_only} retains only loop compression summaries.
27	\cond{VOTA\_no\_ae} is VOTA without assertion-point information.
28	
29	\subsection{Models and Training}
30	
31	We evaluate four models spanning three architecture families: \textbf{Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B/1.5B-Instruct} \citep{qwen2025coder}, \textbf{CodeLlama-13B-Instruct} \citep{roziere2024code}, and \textbf{DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct} \citep{deepseek2024coder}, covering a 1.5B--13B parameter range (8.7$\times$).
32	All conditions use LoRA \citep{hu2022lora} (rank=16, $\alpha$=32, dropout=0.05) on all linear layers, 3 epochs, effective batch size 32, LR $2 \times 10^{-4}$ with linear warmup/decay, max sequence length 2{,}048 tokens.
33	Sequences exceeding this limit are truncated while preserving the verdict label.
34	The primary seed is 42; seeds 123 and 456 test \cond{exception\_only} robustness (Appendix~\ref{sec:training_details}).
35	
36	\subsection{Loss Ablation}
37	
38	To disentangle training signal from input signal quality, we introduce \cond{full\_trace\_label\_only}: identical full-trace input but loss computed only on the verdict token (trace positions masked).
39	Improvement over standard \cond{full\_trace} is attributable to backward-pass signal dilution; the remaining gap to VOTA reflects inference-time input complexity.
40	
41	\begin{table}[t]
42	\centering
43	\caption{Component ablation on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$). Tier~1 signals achieve near-perfect accuracy; combining them (VOTA) slightly dilutes performance. Entries marked $^\dag$/$^\S$/$^\ddag$ are 3-seed mean$\pm$std; per-seed breakdowns in Appendix~\ref{sec:full_results}. Seed $\sigma$ negatively correlates with density (0.00 for exception to 0.52 for loop).}
44	\label{tab:ablation}
45	\small
46	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
47	\begin{tabular}{llccc}
48	\toprule
49	\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Components} & \textbf{Acc (\%)} & \textbf{95\% CI} & \textbf{Tier} \\
50	\midrule
51	ae\_only & assertion-point & 99.64$^\dag$ & [99.26, 99.87] & 1 \\
52	exception\_only & exception events & \textbf{99.83} & [99.58, 100.00] & 1 \\
53	VOTA & exc+ae+loop & 99.05$^\S$ & [98.49, 99.50] & --- \\
54	VOTA\_no\_ae & exc+loop & 99.41 & [98.90, 99.80] & --- \\
55	loop\_only & loop compression & 86.05$^\ddag$ & [83.97, 87.96] & 2 \\
56	full\_trace & complete trace & 81.76 & [79.70, 83.90] & --- \\
57	no\_trace & none & 75.13 & [72.77, 77.48] & --- \\
58	\midrule
59	Regex (best) & exc.\ type regex & 93.61 & --- & --- \\
60	3-shot (Qwen-7B) & in-context learning & 90.84 & --- & --- \\
61	Zero-shot (4 models) & prompting & 51.0--93.5 & --- & --- \\
62	\bottomrule
63	\end{tabular}%
64	}
65	\end{table}

      "content": "\\section{Method}\n\n\\subsection{Task Definition}\n\nWe study \\emph{verdict prediction}: given a Python function, a test input, and an optional execution trace, predict whether the test passes or fails.\nThe model produces a single token (\\textit{pass} or \\textit{fail}), making this a binary classification task.\nUnlike code generation \\citep{chen2025tracepile} or output prediction \\citep{gu2024cruxeval}, verdict prediction isolates the model's ability to detect faults from its ability to simulate execution.\nThe discrete binary outcome makes the effect of each trace component directly measurable, providing cleaner ablation contrasts than generation tasks where output quality varies continuously.\n\n\\subsection{Dataset}\n\\label{sec:dataset}\n\nWe sampled 10 candidate solutions per problem from Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct (temperature 0.8, top\\_p 0.95) for 591 problems from HumanEval \\citep{chen2021evaluating} (164) and MBPP-sanitized \\citep{austin2021program} (427), executed each against canonical tests with \\textit{sys.settrace} to obtain line-level traces and pass/fail verdicts, yielding 5,910 pairs (67\\% pass, 33\\% fail).\nThe dataset is split 80/20 by problem ID into 4,720 training and 1,190 test samples (majority baseline: 71.51\\%).\nThe \\cond{exception\\_only} format collapses passing solutions into identical rows (39.45\\% train, 48.99\\% test duplicates); these duplicates arise from multiple candidate solutions per problem, with the train--test split enforced at the problem level (472 train / 119 test problems, zero crossover; Appendix~\\ref{sec:dedup_stats}).\n\n\\subsection{Trace Representations}\n\nWe define seven conditions that vary the trace information available.\n\\cond{no\\_trace} provides only source code and test input.\n\\cond{full\\_trace} includes the complete line-by-line trace with variable states.\n\\cond{VOTA} combines exception events, assertion-point comparisons, and loop compression (Appendix~\\ref{sec:vota_algorithm}).\nThe remaining conditions isolate individual VOTA components:\n\\cond{exception\\_only} retains only exception and error events;\n\\cond{ae\\_only} retains only assertion-point actual/expected pairs;\n\\cond{loop\\_only} retains only loop compression summaries.\n\\cond{VOTA\\_no\\_ae} is VOTA without assertion-point information.\n\n\\subsection{Models and Training}\n\nWe evaluate four models spanning three architecture families: \\textbf{Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B/1.5B-Instruct} \\citep{qwen2025coder}, \\textbf{CodeLlama-13B-Instruct} \\citep{roziere2024code}, and \\textbf{DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct} \\citep{deepseek2024coder}, covering a 1.5B--13B parameter range (8.7$\\times$).\nAll conditions use LoRA \\citep{hu2022lora} (rank=16, $\\alpha$=32, dropout=0.05) on all linear layers, 3 epochs, effective batch size 32, LR $2 \\times 10^{-4}$ with linear warmup/decay, max sequence length 2{,}048 tokens.\nSequences exceeding this limit are truncated while preserving the verdict label.\nThe primary seed is 42; seeds 123 and 456 test \\cond{exception\\_only} robustness (Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_details}).\n\n\\subsection{Loss Ablation}\n\nTo disentangle training signal from input signal quality, we introduce \\cond{full\\_trace\\_label\\_only}: identical full-trace input but loss computed only on the verdict token (trace positions masked).\nImprovement over standard \\cond{full\\_trace} is attributable to backward-pass signal dilution; the remaining gap to VOTA reflects inference-time input complexity.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Component ablation on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$). Tier~1 signals achieve near-perfect accuracy; combining them (VOTA) slightly dilutes performance. Entries marked $^\\dag$/$^\\S$/$^\\ddag$ are 3-seed mean$\\pm$std; per-seed breakdowns in Appendix~\\ref{sec:full_results}. Seed $\\sigma$ negatively correlates with density (0.00 for exception to 0.52 for loop).}\n\\label{tab:ablation}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Components} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{Tier} \\\\\n\\midrule\nae\\_only & assertion-point & 99.64$^\\dag$ & [99.26, 99.87] & 1 \\\\\nexception\\_only & exception events & \\textbf{99.83} & [99.58, 100.00] & 1 \\\\\nVOTA & exc+ae+loop & 99.05$^\\S$ & [98.49, 99.50] & --- \\\\\nVOTA\\_no\\_ae & exc+loop & 99.41 & [98.90, 99.80] & --- \\\\\nloop\\_only & loop compression & 86.05$^\\ddag$ & [83.97, 87.96] & 2 \\\\\nfull\\_trace & complete trace & 81.76 & [79.70, 83.90] & --- \\\\\nno\\_trace & none & 75.13 & [72.77, 77.48] & --- \\\\\n\\midrule\nRegex (best) & exc.\\ type regex & 93.61 & --- & --- \\\\\n3-shot (Qwen-7B) & in-context learning & 90.84 & --- & --- \\\\\nZero-shot (4 models) & prompting & 51.0--93.5 & --- & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n",
      "numLines": 66,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 66
    }

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	
3	We evaluate two questions:
4	(1)~Do exception signals generalize across model architectures and scales?
5	(2)~What is the relative contribution of each trace component?
6	All experiments use $N{=}1{,}190$ test samples with Wilson 95\% CIs.\footnote{Data is partitioned at the problem level (472 train / 119 test problems, zero crossover; \S\ref{sec:dataset}). The \cond{exception\_only} format collapses passing solutions into identical rows (48.99\% test duplicates; Appendix~\ref{sec:dedup_stats}), but these arise from within-problem variants, not cross-set leakage; deduplication sensitivity analysis confirms 0.00pp delta across all 19 experimental conditions.}
7	All cross-tier differences are significant at $p{<}.0001$ (paired bootstrap, $B{=}10{,}000$) unless noted.
8	Under problem-level clustered bootstrap (effective $N{\approx}119$), cross-tier gaps remain significant ($p{<}.001$); within-tier comparisons show partially overlapping CIs.
9	
10	\subsection{Cross-Model Comparison}
11	\label{sec:cross_model}
12	
13	Table~\ref{tab:cross_model} compares four models spanning three architecture families across a 1.5B--13B parameter range.
14	
15	\paragraph{Exception dominance across families.}
16	Under \cond{exception\_only}, all four models converge to ${\geq}$99.33\% across all seeds, with a cross-model range of just 0.50pp---the tightest of any condition.
17	CodeLlama-13B reaches 100\% on one seed, confirming that the two persistent errors on Qwen (both \textit{GeneratorExit}; Appendix~\ref{sec:irreducible}) are convergence artifacts rather than hard data limitations.
18	
19	\paragraph{Scale invariance.}
20	Exception accuracy is independent of model scale: the smallest model (1.5B) matches the largest (13B) on the same representation (Figure~\ref{fig:cross_scale}).
21	In contrast, \cond{no\_trace} scales monotonically with capacity and exhibits the highest seed variance of any condition.
22	Exception events eliminate both the scale gap and the instability.
23	
24	\paragraph{VOTA robustness.}
25	Excluding one training failure caused by tokenizer-induced truncation (Appendix~\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}), VOTA achieves ${\geq}$98.57\% across all models within a 0.80pp range.
26	The failure is itself informative: 91.7\% of that seed's training samples were truncated, while compact \cond{exception\_only} inputs (median 150 tokens) sidestep truncation entirely.
27	
28	\subsection{Component Ablation}
29	\label{sec:ablation}
30	
31	Table~\ref{tab:ablation} decomposes VOTA on Qwen-2.5-7B, revealing a two-tier hierarchy.
32	Tier~1 signals are individually sufficient: \cond{exception\_only} alone exceeds full VOTA, implying that combining additional components introduces noise rather than value.
33	Removing assertion points has no effect (\cond{VOTA\_no\_ae}), confirming exception dominance.
34	The Tier~1/2 boundary is sharp (Figure~\ref{fig:forest_plot}): the weakest Tier~1 condition exceeds the strongest Tier~2 by over 12pp with non-overlapping confidence intervals.
35	Tier~2 (\cond{loop\_only}) benefits are model-dependent: Qwen-7B gains +10.92pp over \cond{no\_trace} while CodeLlama gains only +0.93pp (n.s.), suggesting loop utilization reflects architectural capacity to exploit structured iteration patterns rather than a universal signal.
36	Within Tier~2, the larger Qwen-7B outgains Qwen-1.5B (+10.92 vs.\ +7.19pp, $p{<}.0001$), the only condition where scale provides a meaningful advantage.
37	
38	\paragraph{Baselines.}
39	The strongest regex heuristic (exception class name matching) trails SFT \cond{exception\_only} by 6.22pp ($p{<}.001$); the gap concentrates on boundary cases where SFT distinguishes caught from uncaught exceptions, correctly resolving 6/8 ambiguous samples vs.\ 0/8 for regex (Appendix~\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).
40	Zero-shot prompting spans a 42pp range across models; SFT compresses this to 0.50pp with 4,720 training samples (Figure~\ref{fig:zeroshot_vs_sft}; Appendix~\ref{sec:zeroshot_cross_model}).
41	
42	\begin{figure}[t]
43	\centering
44	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{forest_plot_tier_boundary}
45	\caption{Cross-model accuracy with 95\% Wilson CIs across all conditions and four models. The shaded region highlights the Tier~1/2 boundary gap ({>}12pp). Within Tier~1, all models converge to $\geq$99.08\%; within Tier~2, performance is model-dependent.}
46	\label{fig:forest_plot}
47	\end{figure}
48	
49	\begin{figure*}[t]
50	\centering
51	\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\textwidth}
52	\centering
53	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{budget_curve}
54	\end{minipage}\hfill
55	\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\textwidth}
56	\centering
57	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{token_distribution}
58	\end{minipage}
59	\caption{(\textbf{Left}) Cross-model tail-truncation budget curve ($N{=}1{,}190$, four models). All models remain near baseline at tail-256/384, then jump sharply at 384$\to$512 as the exception traceback is captured (shaded). Dashed: \cond{exception\_only} range; dotted: \cond{no\_trace} range. (\textbf{Right}) Input token length distribution across trace conditions (training set, $N{=}4{,}720$). Dashed line: 2,048-token context window. \cond{exception\_only} inputs (median 150 tokens) fit entirely within the window, while \cond{full\_trace} inputs frequently exceed it, triggering truncation.}
60	\label{fig:trace_length}
61	\label{fig:token_distribution}
62	\end{figure*}
63	
64	\subsection{Format Marker Ablation}
65	\label{sec:nomarker}
66	
67	The \cond{exception\_only} format includes a ``[No exception data in trace]'' marker for pass-class samples.
68	Retraining with all format markers removed (\cond{nomarker}) yields a modest drop (${\sim}$2pp on both Qwen-7B and Qwen-1.5B), accounting for only ${\sim}$9\% of the total exception lift over \cond{no\_trace}.
69	Exception events thus provide two signal layers: structural markers (${\sim}$2pp, regex-learnable) and semantic content (${\sim}$22pp, requiring SFT to distinguish caught from uncaught exceptions).
70	Both layers show no degradation at smaller scale.
71	
72	\subsection{Information Density Decomposition}
73	\label{sec:density}
74	
75	The gap between \cond{full\_trace} and VOTA decomposes via \cond{full\_trace\_label\_only}, which retains the full trace input but masks all trace tokens from the loss (Table~\ref{tab:density}).
76	
77	\begin{table}[t]
78	\centering
79	\caption{Information density decomposition on Qwen-7B. Gradient masking (\cond{label\_only}) places an upper bound on backward-pass dilution; the remaining gap reflects inference-time input factors. 3-seed means.}
80	\label{tab:density}
81	\small
82	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
83	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
84	\toprule
85	\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Acc (\%)} & \textbf{$\Delta$ vs.\ full\_trace} & \textbf{Gap explained} \\
86	\midrule
87	full\_trace & 81.76 & --- & --- \\
88	label\_only & 85.94{\scriptsize$\pm$1.12} & +4.18pp & 24\% \\
89	VOTA & 99.05{\scriptsize$\pm$0.38} & +17.29pp & 100\% \\
90	\bottomrule
91	\end{tabular}%
92	}
93	\end{table}
94	
95	Gradient masking places an upper bound on backward-pass dilution: the +4.18pp gain bounds the backward-pass contribution to ${\leq}$24\% of the full gap.
96	The remaining majority of the gap persists even with identical loss computation, suggesting that interference from thousands of irrelevant trace lines at inference---rather than training signal dilution---is the dominant factor.
97	Cross-model evidence corroborates this: the tail-truncation phase transition at ${\sim}$500 tokens (\S\ref{sec:trace_length}) coincides with exception traceback length, and cross-scale validation on Qwen-1.5B yields a consistent pattern (gradient masking explains a similar minority share).
98	CodeLlama and DeepSeek exhibit label-only collapse, limiting the decomposition to models whose tokenizers preserve sufficient trace structure.
99	Error analysis confirms all \cond{full\_trace} errors occur where truncation removes diagnostic markers (Figure~\ref{fig:token_distribution}).
100	The two factors may interact, and the decomposition is approximate (Qwen family, 2 scales, 3 seeds).
101	
102	\subsection{Additional Analyses}
103	\label{sec:additional}
104	
105	\paragraph{Tail-truncation budget curve.}
106	\label{sec:trace_length}
107	A cross-model budget sweep reveals a sharp phase transition between 384 and 512 tail tokens (Figure~\ref{fig:trace_length}).
108	Below this threshold, all models remain near baseline regardless of how many tokens are retained.
109	Above it, accuracy jumps by 13--22pp in a single step, converging into a narrow band by tail-1024.
110	The transition zone coincides with the typical exception traceback length (${\sim}$400--500 tokens), providing spatial evidence that diagnostic events in the trace tail drive the accuracy gains.
111	
112	Tail-truncation dramatically outperforms head-truncation: retaining the last $k$ tokens exceeds retaining the first $k$ by 15--23pp across models.
113	Under head-truncation, accuracy is non-monotonic and confined to a narrow band, because mid-length prefixes retain enough irrelevant content to mislead the model while discarding diagnostic events near the trace terminus (Appendix~\ref{sec:trace_length_full}).
114	
115	\paragraph{Calibration.}
116	\label{sec:calibration}
117	Calibration mirrors the tier hierarchy: \cond{exception\_only} achieves ECE\,=\,0.003 vs.\ 0.171 for \cond{no\_trace}, a 63$\times$ gap (Figure~\ref{fig:calibration}).
118	This advantage generalizes across all four models (ECE~${<}$~0.007; Appendix~\ref{sec:calibration_full}).
119	
120	\paragraph{Error orthogonality and exception types.}
121	\cond{exception\_only}'s residual errors have zero overlap with all other conditions' error sets (Jaccard\,=\,0.000; Figure~\ref{fig:error_overlap}), which confirms it operates on an independent signal dimension.
122	All standard exception types achieve 100\% accuracy; the only failures arise from \textit{GeneratorExit}, a benign \textit{BaseException} subclass (Appendix~\ref{sec:exception_type}).
123	
124	\paragraph{Cross-dataset generalization.}
125	\label{sec:debugbench}
126	DebugBench \citep{tian2024debugbench} ($N{=}426$) preserves the component ranking (\cond{exception\_only} 100\%, VOTA 99.77\%, \cond{no\_trace} 96.71\%), though narrow margins reflect a ceiling effect from structurally distinctive mutations (Appendix~\ref{sec:debugbench_full}).
127

    "file": {
      "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_4/projects/trace-verdict-study/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
      "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\nWe evaluate two questions:\n(1)~Do exception signals generalize across model architectures and scales?\n(2)~What is the relative contribution of each trace component?\nAll experiments use $N{=}1{,}190$ test samples with Wilson 95\\% CIs.\\footnote{Data is partitioned at the problem level (472 train / 119 test problems, zero crossover; \\S\\ref{sec:dataset}). The \\cond{exception\\_only} format collapses passing solutions into identical rows (48.99\\% test duplicates; Appendix~\\ref{sec:dedup_stats}), but these arise from within-problem variants, not cross-set leakage; deduplication sensitivity analysis confirms 0.00pp delta across all 19 experimental conditions.}\nAll cross-tier differences are significant at $p{<}.0001$ (paired bootstrap, $B{=}10{,}000$) unless noted.\nUnder problem-level clustered bootstrap (effective $N{\\approx}119$), cross-tier gaps remain significant ($p{<}.001$); within-tier comparisons show partially overlapping CIs.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Comparison}\n\\label{sec:cross_model}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_model} compares four models spanning three architecture families across a 1.5B--13B parameter range.\n\n\\paragraph{Exception dominance across families.}\nUnder \\cond{exception\\_only}, all four models converge to ${\\geq}$99.33\\% across all seeds, with a cross-model range of just 0.50pp---the tightest of any condition.\nCodeLlama-13B reaches 100\\% on one seed, confirming that the two persistent errors on Qwen (both \\textit{GeneratorExit}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:irreducible}) are convergence artifacts rather than hard data limitations.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale invariance.}\nException accuracy is independent of model scale: the smallest model (1.5B) matches the largest (13B) on the same representation (Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_scale}).\nIn contrast, \\cond{no\\_trace} scales monotonically with capacity and exhibits the highest seed variance of any condition.\nException events eliminate both the scale gap and the instability.\n\n\\paragraph{VOTA robustness.}\nExcluding one training failure caused by tokenizer-induced truncation (Appendix~\\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}), VOTA achieves ${\\geq}$98.57\\% across all models within a 0.80pp range.\nThe failure is itself informative: 91.7\\% of that seed's training samples were truncated, while compact \\cond{exception\\_only} inputs (median 150 tokens) sidestep truncation entirely.\n\n\\subsection{Component Ablation}\n\\label{sec:ablation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:ablation} decomposes VOTA on Qwen-2.5-7B, revealing a two-tier hierarchy.\nTier~1 signals are individually sufficient: \\cond{exception\\_only} alone exceeds full VOTA, implying that combining additional components introduces noise rather than value.\nRemoving assertion points has no effect (\\cond{VOTA\\_no\\_ae}), confirming exception dominance.\nThe Tier~1/2 boundary is sharp (Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot}): the weakest Tier~1 condition exceeds the strongest Tier~2 by over 12pp with non-overlapping confidence intervals.\nTier~2 (\\cond{loop\\_only}) benefits are model-dependent: Qwen-7B gains +10.92pp over \\cond{no\\_trace} while CodeLlama gains only +0.93pp (n.s.), suggesting loop utilization reflects architectural capacity to exploit structured iteration patterns rather than a universal signal.\nWithin Tier~2, the larger Qwen-7B outgains Qwen-1.5B (+10.92 vs.\\ +7.19pp, $p{<}.0001$), the only condition where scale provides a meaningful advantage.\n\n\\paragraph{Baselines.}\nThe strongest regex heuristic (exception class name matching) trails SFT \\cond{exception\\_only} by 6.22pp ($p{<}.001$); the gap concentrates on boundary cases where SFT distinguishes caught from uncaught exceptions, correctly resolving 6/8 ambiguous samples vs.\\ 0/8 for regex (Appendix~\\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).\nZero-shot prompting spans a 42pp range across models; SFT compresses this to 0.50pp with 4,720 training samples (Figure~\\ref{fig:zeroshot_vs_sft}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:zeroshot_cross_model}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{forest_plot_tier_boundary}\n\\caption{Cross-model accuracy with 95\\% Wilson CIs across all conditions and four models. The shaded region highlights the Tier~1/2 boundary gap ({>}12pp). Within Tier~1, all models converge to $\\geq$99.08\\%; within Tier~2, performance is model-dependent.}\n\\label{fig:forest_plot}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\\textwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{budget_curve}\n\\end{minipage}\\hfill\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\\textwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{token_distribution}\n\\end{minipage}\n\\caption{(\\textbf{Left}) Cross-model tail-truncation budget curve ($N{=}1{,}190$, four models). All models remain near baseline at tail-256/384, then jump sharply at 384$\\to$512 as the exception traceback is captured (shaded). Dashed: \\cond{exception\\_only} range; dotted: \\cond{no\\_trace} range. (\\textbf{Right}) Input token length distribution across trace conditions (training set, $N{=}4{,}720$). Dashed line: 2,048-token context window. \\cond{exception\\_only} inputs (median 150 tokens) fit entirely within the window, while \\cond{full\\_trace} inputs frequently exceed it, triggering truncation.}\n\\label{fig:trace_length}\n\\label{fig:token_distribution}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Format Marker Ablation}\n\\label{sec:nomarker}\n\nThe \\cond{exception\\_only} format includes a ``[No exception data in trace]'' marker for pass-class samples.\nRetraining with all format markers removed (\\cond{nomarker}) yields a modest drop (${\\sim}$2pp on both Qwen-7B and Qwen-1.5B), accounting for only ${\\sim}$9\\% of the total exception lift over \\cond{no\\_trace}.\nException events thus provide two signal layers: structural markers (${\\sim}$2pp, regex-learnable) and semantic content (${\\sim}$22pp, requiring SFT to distinguish caught from uncaught exceptions).\nBoth layers show no degradation at smaller scale.\n\n\\subsection{Information Density Decomposition}\n\\label{sec:density}\n\nThe gap between \\cond{full\\_trace} and VOTA decomposes via \\cond{full\\_trace\\_label\\_only}, which retains the full trace input but masks all trace tokens from the loss (Table~\\ref{tab:density}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Information density decomposition on Qwen-7B. Gradient masking (\\cond{label\\_only}) places an upper bound on backward-pass dilution; the remaining gap reflects inference-time input factors. 3-seed means.}\n\\label{tab:density}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ vs.\\ full\\_trace} & \\textbf{Gap explained} \\\\\n\\midrule\nfull\\_trace & 81.76 & --- & --- \\\\\nlabel\\_only & 85.94{\\scriptsize$\\pm$1.12} & +4.18pp & 24\\% \\\\\nVOTA & 99.05{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.38} & +17.29pp & 100\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\nGradient masking places an upper bound on backward-pass dilution: the +4.18pp gain bounds the backward-pass contribution to ${\\leq}$24\\% of the full gap.\nThe remaining majority of the gap persists even with identical loss computation, suggesting that interference from thousands of irrelevant trace lines at inference---rather than training signal dilution---is the dominant factor.\nCross-model evidence corroborates this: the tail-truncation phase transition at ${\\sim}$500 tokens (\\S\\ref{sec:trace_length}) coincides with exception traceback length, and cross-scale validation on Qwen-1.5B yields a consistent pattern (gradient masking explains a similar minority share).\nCodeLlama and DeepSeek exhibit label-only collapse, limiting the decomposition to models whose tokenizers preserve sufficient trace structure.\nError analysis confirms all \\cond{full\\_trace} errors occur where truncation removes diagnostic markers (Figure~\\ref{fig:token_distribution}).\nThe two factors may interact, and the decomposition is approximate (Qwen family, 2 scales, 3 seeds).\n\n\\subsection{Additional Analyses}\n\\label{sec:additional}\n\n\\paragraph{Tail-truncation budget curve.}\n\\label{sec:trace_length}\nA cross-model budget sweep reveals a sharp phase transition between 384 and 512 tail tokens (Figure~\\ref{fig:trace_length}).\nBelow this threshold, all models remain near baseline regardless of how many tokens are retained.\nAbove it, accuracy jumps by 13--22pp in a single step, converging into a narrow band by tail-1024.\nThe transition zone coincides with the typical exception traceback length (${\\sim}$400--500 tokens), providing spatial evidence that diagnostic events in the trace tail drive the accuracy gains.\n\nTail-truncation dramatically outperforms head-truncation: retaining the last $k$ tokens exceeds retaining the first $k$ by 15--23pp across models.\nUnder head-truncation, accuracy is non-monotonic and confined to a narrow band, because mid-length prefixes retain enough irrelevant content to mislead the model while discarding diagnostic events near the trace terminus (Appendix~\\ref{sec:trace_length_full}).\n\n\\paragraph{Calibration.}\n\\label{sec:calibration}\nCalibration mirrors the tier hierarchy: \\cond{exception\\_only} achieves ECE\\,=\\,0.003 vs.\\ 0.171 for \\cond{no\\_trace}, a 63$\\times$ gap (Figure~\\ref{fig:calibration}).\nThis advantage generalizes across all four models (ECE~${<}$~0.007; Appendix~\\ref{sec:calibration_full}).\n\n\\paragraph{Error orthogonality and exception types.}\n\\cond{exception\\_only}'s residual errors have zero overlap with all other conditions' error sets (Jaccard\\,=\\,0.000; Figure~\\ref{fig:error_overlap}), which confirms it operates on an independent signal dimension.\nAll standard exception types achieve 100\\% accuracy; the only failures arise from \\textit{GeneratorExit}, a benign \\textit{BaseException} subclass (Appendix~\\ref{sec:exception_type}).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-dataset generalization.}\n\\label{sec:debugbench}\nDebugBench \\citep{tian2024debugbench} ($N{=}426$) preserves the component ranking (\\cond{exception\\_only} 100\\%, VOTA 99.77\\%, \\cond{no\\_trace} 96.71\\%), though narrow margins reflect a ceiling effect from structurally distinctive mutations (Appendix~\\ref{sec:debugbench_full}).\n\n",
      "numLines": 128,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 128
    }
  },

1	\documentclass[11pt,a4paper]{article}

16	\newcommand{\cond}[1]{\textit{#1}}
17	
18	\title{Trace Verbosity Degrades SFT Verdict Prediction:\\ A Case Study in Information Density Effects}
19	\author{Anonymous ACL submission}
20	
21	\begin{document}
22	\maketitle
23	
24	\begin{abstract}
25	A natural assumption when augmenting code models with execution traces is that richer runtime information yields better predictions.
26	We challenge this for verdict prediction---classifying whether a test passes or fails---and find the opposite: complete traces degrade accuracy to near-baseline levels, while compact exception events alone reach ${\geq}$99.33\% across all models tested.
27	Through the first systematic component ablation of trace representations across multiple code models and architecture families, we identify information density as the key factor: exception events encode the fault signal in roughly 150 tokens, whereas full traces dilute it across thousands of routine steps, trapping models in unstable oscillation.
28	Controlled experiments support this finding: gradient masking isolates backward-pass dilution as a minor factor, a tail-truncation sweep reveals a sharp phase transition at the diagnostic-event boundary, and format-marker removal confirms the signal is semantic.
29	These results decompose trace verbosity into a two-tier diagnostic hierarchy and establish that targeted signal extraction strictly dominates richer input for structurally constrained tasks\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/trace-verdict-study-291F/}.}.
30	\end{abstract}
31	
32	\input{introduction}
33	\input{related_work}
34	\input{method}
35	\input{experiments}
36	\input{discussion}
37	\input{conclusion}
38	
39	\section*{Limitations}
40	
41	Our study has three main limitations.
42	First, all experiments use Python execution traces from code-specialized models ranging from 1.5B to 13B parameters; whether the observed density--efficiency relationship holds for other programming languages, human-written code, or larger models (30B+) remains untested.
43	Second, our findings may not generalize beyond the current setting: Python's structured exception hierarchy makes exception events particularly informative, and languages with less structured error reporting may exhibit different signal distributions.
44	Third, the gradient masking experiment isolates backward-pass signal dilution but does not quantify forward-pass attention effects; disentangling these two factors requires further investigation.
45	
46	\bibliography{references}
47	
48	\appendix
49	\input{appendix}
50	
51	\end{document}
```

252	%% ============================================================
253	\section{Tokenizer and Truncation Analysis}
254	\label{sec:tokenizer_analysis}
255	
256	DeepSeek VOTA seed 42 (84.45\%) is a training failure driven by tokenizer-induced truncation.
257	Re-evaluation of the seed 42 checkpoint with the standard max\_length=2048 yields 16\% accuracy, which confirms complete model collapse rather than marginal degradation.
258	The previously reported 84.45\% was obtained with max\_length=16384, which partially bypasses tokenizer truncation.
259	Seeds 123 and 456, evaluated under identical conditions, achieve 99.16\% and 99.33\% respectively.
260	The DeepSeek tokenizer averages 2{,}626 tokens per VOTA sample versus 1{,}929 for Qwen, causing 91.7\% of DeepSeek VOTA training samples to exceed the 2{,}048-token context window.
261	The training framework preserves the verdict label but truncates trace content, so the model trains with incomplete diagnostic information for the majority of samples.
262	Seeds 123 and 456 (99.16\% and 99.33\%) demonstrate that DeepSeek can learn VOTA when LoRA initialization avoids the truncation-sensitive basin; the 2-seed mean (99.25\%$\pm$0.12) matches the cross-model VOTA range (98.74--99.54\%).
263	
264	A post-hoc group analysis on seed 42 provides directional evidence: partitioning test samples by whether their training-set counterparts exceeded the context window, intact samples ($N{=}99$) achieve 100\% accuracy while truncated samples ($N{=}1{,}091$) achieve 83.04\% (95\% CI [80.75\%, 85.24\%]; Fisher exact $p{<}.001$), with all 185 errors concentrated in the fail class (fail recall = 31.23\%).
265	Per-class analysis (Table~\ref{tab:per_class}) confirms that seed 42 achieves 100\% fail precision but only 45.43\% fail recall, systematically defaulting to \texttt{pass}.
266	
267	Seed robustness is consistent across models: Qwen \texttt{exception\_only} produces identical 99.83\% across all three seeds ($\sigma{=}0$), and DeepSeek achieves 99.39\%$\pm$0.10 (seeds 42/456: 99.33\%, seed 123: 99.50\%).
268	Because \texttt{exception\_only} test inputs contain 48.99\% duplicates (607 unique of 1,190; \S\ref{sec:dataset}), CIs assume $n{=}1{,}190$ independent trials; under deduplication ($n{=}607$), Qwen's accuracy is 99.67\% (Wilson CI [98.81, 99.91]).
269	
270	%% ============================================================
271	\section{Irreducible Sample Analysis}
272	\label{sec:irreducible}
273	
274	The \texttt{exception\_only} condition with Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B misclassifies exactly 2 out of 1,190 test samples across all three seeds (42, 123, 456), with 100\% overlap across seeds.

    "oldString": "We define seven conditions that vary the trace information available.\n\\cond{no\\_trace} provides only source code and test input.\n\\cond{full\\_trace} includes the complete line-by-line trace with variable states.\n\\cond{VOTA} combines exception events, assertion-point comparisons, and loop compression (Appendix~\\ref{sec:vota_algorithm}).\nThe remaining conditions isolate individual VOTA components:\n\\cond{exception\\_only} retains only exception and error events;\n\\cond{ae\\_only} retains only assertion-point actual/expected pairs;\n\\cond{loop\\_only} retains only loop compression summaries.\n\\cond{VOTA\\_no\\_ae} is VOTA without assertion-point information.",
    "newString": "We define seven conditions that vary the trace information available:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1em]\n\\item \\cond{no\\_trace}: source code and test input only.\n\\item \\cond{full\\_trace}: complete line-by-line trace with variable states.\n\\item \\cond{VOTA}: exception events + assertion-point comparisons + loop compression (Appendix~\\ref{sec:vota_algorithm}).\n\\item \\cond{exception\\_only}: exception and error events only.\n\\item \\cond{ae\\_only}: assertion-point actual/expected pairs only.\n\\item \\cond{loop\\_only}: loop compression summaries only.\n\\item \\cond{VOTA\\_no\\_ae}: VOTA without assertion-point information.\n\\end{itemize}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\n\\subsection{Task Definition}\n\nWe study \\emph{verdict prediction}: given a Python function, a test input, and an optional execution trace, predict whether the test passes or fails.\nThe model produces a single token (\\textit{pass} or \\textit{fail}), making this a binary classification task.\nUnlike code generation \\citep{chen2025tracepile} or output prediction \\citep{gu2024cruxeval}, verdict prediction isolates the model's ability to detect faults from its ability to simulate execution.\nThe discrete binary outcome makes the effect of each trace component directly measurable, providing cleaner ablation contrasts than generation tasks where output quality varies continuously.\n\n\\subsection{Dataset}\n\\label{sec:dataset}\n\nWe sampled 10 candidate solutions per problem from Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct (temperature 0.8, top\\_p 0.95) for 591 problems from HumanEval \\citep{chen2021evaluating} (164) and MBPP-sanitized \\citep{austin2021program} (427), executed each against canonical tests with \\textit{sys.settrace} to obtain line-level traces and pass/fail verdicts, yielding 5,910 pairs (67\\% pass, 33\\% fail).\nThe dataset is split 80/20 by problem ID into 4,720 training and 1,190 test samples (majority baseline: 71.51\\%).\nThe \\cond{exception\\_only} format collapses passing solutions into identical rows (39.45\\% train, 48.99\\% test duplicates); these duplicates arise from multiple candidate solutions per problem, with the train--test split enforced at the problem level (472 train / 119 test problems, zero crossover; Appendix~\\ref{sec:dedup_stats}).\n\n\\subsection{Trace Representations}\n\nWe define seven conditions that vary the trace information available.\n\\cond{no\\_trace} provides only source code and test input.\n\\cond{full\\_trace} includes the complete line-by-line trace with variable states.\n\\cond{VOTA} combines exception events, assertion-point comparisons, and loop compression (Appendix~\\ref{sec:vota_algorithm}).\nThe remaining conditions isolate individual VOTA components:\n\\cond{exception\\_only} retains only exception and error events;\n\\cond{ae\\_only} retains only assertion-point actual/expected pairs;\n\\cond{loop\\_only} retains only loop compression summaries.\n\\cond{VOTA\\_no\\_ae} is VOTA without assertion-point information.\n\n\\subsection{Models and Training}\n\nWe evaluate four models spanning three architecture families: \\textbf{Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B/1.5B-Instruct} \\citep{qwen2025coder}, \\textbf{CodeLlama-13B-Instruct} \\citep{roziere2024code}, and \\textbf{DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct} \\citep{deepseek2024coder}, covering a 1.5B--13B parameter range (8.7$\\times$).\nAll conditions use LoRA \\citep{hu2022lora} (rank=16, $\\alpha$=32, dropout=0.05) on all linear layers, 3 epochs, effective batch size 32, LR $2 \\times 10^{-4}$ with linear warmup/decay, max sequence length 2{,}048 tokens.\nSequences exceeding this limit are truncated while preserving the verdict label.\nThe primary seed is 42; seeds 123 and 456 test \\cond{exception\\_only} robustness (Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_details}).\n\n\\subsection{Loss Ablation}\n\nTo disentangle training signal from input signal quality, we introduce \\cond{full\\_trace\\_label\\_only}: identical full-trace input but loss computed only on the verdict token (trace positions masked).\nImprovement over standard \\cond{full\\_trace} is attributable to backward-pass signal dilution; the remaining gap to VOTA reflects inference-time input complexity.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Component ablation on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$). Tier~1 signals achieve near-perfect accuracy; combining them (VOTA) slightly dilutes performance. Entries marked $^\\dag$/$^\\S$/$^\\ddag$ are 3-seed mean$\\pm$std; per-seed breakdowns in Appendix~\\ref{sec:full_results}. Seed $\\sigma$ negatively correlates with density (0.00 for exception to 0.52 for loop).}\n\\label{tab:ablation}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Components} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{Tier} \\\\\n\\midrule\nae\\_only & assertion-point & 99.64$^\\dag$ & [99.26, 99.87] & 1 \\\\\nexception\\_only & exception events & \\textbf{99.83} & [99.58, 100.00] & 1 \\\\\nVOTA & exc+ae+loop & 99.05$^\\S$ & [98.49, 99.50] & --- \\\\\nVOTA\\_no\\_ae & exc+loop & 99.41 & [98.90, 99.80] & --- \\\\\nloop\\_only & loop compression & 86.05$^\\ddag$ & [83.97, 87.96] & 2 \\\\\nfull\\_trace & complete trace & 81.76 & [79.70, 83.90] & --- \\\\\nno\\_trace & none & 75.13 & [72.77, 77.48] & --- \\\\\n\\midrule\nRegex (best) & exc.\\ type regex & 93.61 & --- & --- \\\\\n3-shot (Qwen-7B) & in-context learning & 90.84 & --- & --- \\\\\nZero-shot (4 models) & prompting & 51.0--93.5 & --- & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n",

[review])\n\\usepackage{newtxtext,newtxmath}\n\\usepackage[scaled=0.85]{inconsolata}\n\\usepackage{latexsym}\n\\usepackage{amsmath}\n\\usepackage{graphicx}\n\\graphicspath{{../../figures/paper/}}\n\\usepackage{booktabs}\n\\usepackage{multirow}\n\\usepackage{xcolor}\n\\usepackage{algorithm}\n\\usepackage{algorithmic}\n\\usepackage{url}\n\\newcommand{\\cond}[1]{\\textit{#1}}\n\n\\title{Trace Verbosity Degrades SFT Verdict Prediction:\\\\ A Case Study in Information Density Effects}\n\\author{Anonymous ACL submission}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n\\begin{abstract}\nA natural assumption when augmenting code models with execution traces is that richer runtime information yields better predictions.\nWe challenge this for verdict prediction---classifying whether a test passes or fails---and find the opposite: complete traces degrade accuracy to near-baseline levels, while compact exception events alone reach ${\\geq}$99.33\\% across all models tested.\nThrough the first systematic component ablation of trace representations across multiple code models and architecture families, we identify information density as the key factor: exception events encode the fault signal in roughly 150 tokens, whereas full traces dilute it across thousands of routine steps, trapping models in unstable oscillation.\nControlled experiments support this finding: gradient masking isolates backward-pass dilution as a minor factor, a tail-truncation sweep reveals a sharp phase transition at the diagnostic-event boundary, and format-marker removal confirms the signal is semantic.\nThese results decompose trace verbosity into a two-tier diagnostic hierarchy and establish that targeted signal extraction strictly dominates richer input for structurally constrained tasks\\footnote{Code and data are available at \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/trace-verdict-study-291F/}.}.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\input{introduction}\n\\input{related_work}\n\\input{method}\n\\input{experiments}\n\\input{discussion}\n\\input{conclusion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur study has three main limitations.\nFirst, all experiments use Python execution traces from code-specialized models ranging from 1.5B to 13B parameters; whether the observed density--efficiency relationship holds for other programming languages, human-written code, or larger models (30B+) remains untested.\nSecond, our findings may not generalize beyond the current setting: Python's structured exception hierarchy makes exception events particularly informative, and languages with less structured error reporting may exhibit different signal distributions.\nThird, the gradient masking experiment isolates backward-pass signal dilution but does not quantify forward-pass attention effects; disentangling these two factors requires further investigation.\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\appendix\n\\input{appendix}\n\n\\end{document}",

    "oldString": "\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]",
    "newString": "\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\noindent These results hold across four models (1.5B--13B) from three architecture families and transfer to an independent dataset (\\S\\ref{sec:debugbench}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nExecution traces record a program's complete runtime behavior: every function call, variable assignment, and control flow decision.\nRecent work has incorporated them into code language models for tasks from bug localization to program repair \\citep{chen2025tracepile,ding2024traced,wang2025dcsh,ni2024next}, under the working assumption that richer runtime information yields better predictions.\n\\emph{We find the opposite.}\nFor verdict prediction (classifying whether a test passes or fails), feeding a model the complete execution trace produces accuracies of 72--82\\%, barely above a source-code-only baseline, while a single compact component of the same trace reaches ${\\geq}$99.33\\% across all models tested.\nVerbose traces do not just fail to help; they actively impair learning.\n\nVerdict prediction offers a clean testbed for isolating this effect.\nAs binary classification, it separates signal extraction from generation noise; as fault detection, the model need only detect failure, not simulate execution.\nIf the fault signal concentrates in a small trace subset, information density should matter more than quantity.\nPrior work found no single trace representation consistently outperforms others \\citep{wang2025dcsh}, but focused on generation tasks where this density hypothesis could not be cleanly tested.\n\nWe conduct the first systematic component ablation of trace representations, decomposing VOTA (exception events + assertion comparisons + loop compression) into individual components across four models spanning 1.5B to 13B parameters and three architecture families \\citep{qwen2025coder,roziere2024code,deepseek2024coder}, on 591 problems from HumanEval \\citep{chen2021evaluating} and MBPP-sanitized \\citep{austin2021program}.\nThe results are unambiguous: exception events (a median of 150 tokens encoding error type, stack frame, and message) reach ${\\geq}$99.33\\% accuracy across all models and seeds, converging within 50 training steps.\nFull traces oscillate between 82--89\\% without stable convergence even under controlled gradient masking. This confirms that information density, not model capacity, is the binding constraint (Figure~\\ref{fig:training_dynamics}).\n\nThis finding is robust to several potential confounds.\nRemoving format markers preserves accuracy within 2pp, and on 8 boundary samples where exceptions appear in passing code, the fine-tuned model correctly classifies 6/8 vs.\\ 0/8 for a regex baseline, which confirms the signal is semantic (\\S\\ref{sec:nomarker}).\nGradient masking bounds the backward-pass contribution to ${\\leq}$4pp (\\S\\ref{sec:density}), a tail-truncation budget sweep across four models reveals a phase transition at ${\\sim}$500 tokens (\\S\\ref{sec:trace_length}), and cross-dataset transfer to DebugBench ($N{=}426$) preserves the component ranking (\\S\\ref{sec:debugbench}).\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Information density as the binding constraint on SFT learning}: training dynamics, gradient masking (${\\leq}$4pp backward-pass contribution), and cross-model convergence confirm that compact trace inputs learn faster and more stably than verbose ones, independent of model scale (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}, \\S\\ref{sec:density}).\n\\item \\textbf{Exception events as the minimal sufficient signal}: ${\\geq}$99.33\\% across all four models with zero cross-seed variance on Qwen and sub-1pp cross-model range (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_model}).\n\\item \\textbf{A two-tier diagnostic hierarchy with quantitative decomposition}: Tier~1 signals (exceptions) generalize across architectures ($<$1pp range), while Tier~2 (loops) is model-dependent (${\\sim}$9pp range); a phase transition at ${\\sim}$500 tail tokens localizes the diagnostic boundary (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}, \\S\\ref{sec:trace_length}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_cross_model_gap}\n\\caption{Verdict accuracy vs.\\ model scale. \\cond{exception\\_only} maintains ${\\geq}$99.33\\% regardless of scale, while \\cond{no\\_trace} shows monotonic sensitivity. \\cond{nomarker} (markers removed) shows no scale degradation.}\n\\label{fig:cross_scale}\n\\end{figure}\n",

    "oldString": "\\caption{Cross-model verdict accuracy (\\%, $N{=}1{,}190$) across four models (1.5B--13B) and three architecture families. Multi-seed entries report mean$\\pm$std over 2--3 seeds. Bold: best per model. Full per-seed breakdowns in Appendix~\\ref{sec:full_results}; per-class metrics in Appendix~\\ref{sec:per_class}.}",
    "newString": "\\caption{Cross-model verdict accuracy (\\%, $N{=}1{,}190$) across four models and three architecture families. Multi-seed entries report mean$\\pm$std. Bold: best per model. $^\\ddag$2-seed mean (training failure excluded; Appendix~\\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}). Per-seed breakdowns in Appendix~\\ref{sec:full_results}.}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\subsection{Execution Traces for Code LLMs}\n\nRecent work has scaled trace-augmented training: TracePile \\citep{chen2025tracepile} pre-trained on 2.6M execution-annotated samples, while \\citet{liu2023codeexecution} pre-trained models to predict execution outputs from traces.\n\\citet{wang2025dcsh} found trace utility to be task- and format-dependent but did not decompose individual trace components; relative to their work, we contribute a two-tier signal hierarchy, a phase transition in trace-length budgets, calibration analysis, and cross-architecture validation.\nNExT \\citep{ni2024next} and ET-CoT \\citep{eguchi2025etcot} leveraged trace chain-of-thought for execution reasoning, and \\citet{ferrando2025execute} found compact traces comparable to full traces, aligning with our density finding.\nCRUXEval \\citep{gu2024cruxeval}, extended to 19 languages by CRUXEval-X \\citep{xu2025cruxevalx}, established execution reasoning benchmarks, though neither decomposes which trace components contribute to \\emph{classification} accuracy.\n\\citet{shi2023distracted} showed LLMs are easily distracted by irrelevant context, providing a complementary explanation for why verbose traces impair SFT learning.\nIn the prompt compression literature, LLMLingua \\citep{jiang2023llmlingua} and Selective Context \\citep{li2023selectivecontext} showed that pruning low-information tokens preserves downstream accuracy; TraceCoder \\citep{huang2026tracecoder} and InspectCoder \\citep{wang2025inspectcoder} apply selective trace extraction for automated debugging; and \\citet{haque2025traces} found raw traces provide limited repair benefit.\nOur density decomposition complements these methods by quantifying \\emph{why} compression helps (gradient dilution explains ${\\leq}$24\\% of the gap; the remainder reflects inference-time interference).\n\n\\subsection{Verdict Prediction and Fault Detection}\n\nVerdict prediction is an instance of the longstanding test oracle problem \\citep{barr2015oracle}.\nTRACED \\citep{ding2024traced} showed execution-aware pretraining improves vulnerability detection; DeepDFA \\citep{steenhoek2024deepdfa} and learned program slicing \\citep{yadavally2024slicing} combined classical program analysis with deep learning for defect detection.\nIn patch correctness prediction, representation learning of code changes has progressed from CC2Vec \\citep{hoang2020cc2vec} to LLM4PatchCorrect \\citep{zhou2024llm4patch} (84.4\\%) and APPT \\citep{appt2024} (79.7\\%); \\citet{tian2020patchcorrectness} evaluated change representations for predicting repair correctness.\nFuseFL \\citep{widyasari2024fusefl} and \\citet{foster2025mutation} demonstrated that structured fault signals (stack traces, mutation outcomes) improve localization; the equivalent-mutant problem \\citep{papadakis2019mutation} poses an analogous classification challenge.\nSpectrum-based fault localization (SBFL) ranks program elements by suspiciousness from test spectra \\citep{abreu2007accuracy,jones2002visualization,wong2014dstar}; recent LLM-based approaches such as AutoFL \\citep{kang2024autofl} and AgentFL \\citep{qin2024agentfl} share our insight that anomalous execution events carry the strongest diagnostic signal.\nIn agent-based repair, RepairAgent \\citep{bouzenia2025repairagent}, Agentless \\citep{xia2024agentless}, SWE-agent \\citep{yang2024sweagent}, and OpenHands \\citep{wang2025openhands} all incorporate execution feedback in repair workflows; in the SWE-bench ecosystem \\citep{jimenez2024swebench}, these agents rely on test verdict signals to guide patch selection, yet which trace components carry that signal most efficiently remains unstudied.\nLDB \\citep{zhong2024ldb} applies segment-level trace verification for debugging, and SelfDebug \\citep{chen2024selfdebug} and Reflexion \\citep{shinn2023reflexion} iteratively refine code using execution outcomes.\nNone of these works isolate individual trace components or compare against rule-based baselines.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-model verdict accuracy (\\%, $N{=}1{,}190$) across four models (1.5B--13B) and three architecture families. Multi-seed entries report mean$\\pm$std over 2--3 seeds. Bold: best per model. Full per-seed breakdowns in Appendix~\\ref{sec:full_results}; per-class metrics in Appendix~\\ref{sec:per_class}.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Qwen-7B} & \\textbf{Qwen-1.5B} & \\textbf{CL-13B} & \\textbf{DS-6.7B} \\\\\n\\midrule\nexception\\_only & \\textbf{99.83}{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.00} & \\textbf{99.83}{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.00} & \\textbf{99.89}{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.10} & \\textbf{99.39}{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.10} \\\\\nae\\_only & 99.64{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.49} & 98.83{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.36} & 99.08{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.00} & 98.53{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.30} \\\\\nVOTA & 99.05{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.38} & 99.54{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.06} & 98.74{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.24} & 99.25$^\\ddag$ \\\\\nloop\\_only & 86.05{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.52} & 79.54{\\scriptsize$\\pm$1.42} & 77.40{\\scriptsize$\\pm$1.54} & 80.31{\\scriptsize$\\pm$1.65} \\\\\nno\\_trace & 75.13 & 72.35{\\scriptsize$\\pm$4.48} & 76.47 & 73.36 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMajority baseline & \\multicolumn{4}{c}{71.51} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\par\\raggedright\\footnotesize\nDeepSeek \\cond{exception\\_only} 3 seeds: 99.33/99.50/99.33 ($\\sigma{=}$0.10pp).\nVOTA on Qwen-7B: 99.41/98.66/99.08 ($\\sigma{=}$0.38).\n\\cond{ae\\_only} 3 seeds: 99.92/99.08/99.92 (mean 99.64\\%); Qwen-7B \\cond{loop\\_only} 3 seeds: 85.63/85.88/86.64 (mean 86.05\\%).\nQwen-1.5B \\cond{exception\\_only}: 99.83\\% across 3 seeds ($\\sigma{=}$0).\n$^\\ddag$DeepSeek VOTA 3 seeds: 84.45/99.16/99.33; seed 42 (84.45\\%, fail recall\\,=\\,45.4\\%) is a training failure due to tokenizer-driven truncation (Appendix~\\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}), reported as 2-seed mean.\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Calibration and Training Dynamics}\n\nNeural network calibration \\citep{guo2017calibration}, extended to LLMs \\citep{kadavath2022language,geng2024calibration}, studies confidence--correctness alignment; \\citet{spiess2025calibration} specifically addressed calibration for code models.\nOur ECE ${<}$ 0.007 across four models suggests compact inputs improve calibration beyond accuracy gains alone.\nJust-in-time defect prediction \\citep{kamei2013large,hoang2019deepjit,pornprasit2021jitline} shares our fault detection motivation but operates on code change features rather than execution traces; our work complements this line by showing that execution-time fault signals can be classified with near-perfect accuracy when properly extracted.\nCurriculum learning \\citep{bengio2009curriculum}, dataset cartography via training dynamics \\citep{swayamdipta2020cartography}, and data selection methods \\citep{sachdeva2024dataefficient,xie2023doremi} demonstrate that data quality and ordering improve convergence---consistent with our finding that compact exceptions enable convergence within 50 steps while verbose traces trap the model in oscillation across all 444 training steps.\n\\citet{yang2024generalization} further showed that fine-tuned LLMs generalize better on concise inputs, providing a complementary perspective on why information density governs SFT effectiveness.\n\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\nWe evaluate two questions:\n(1)~Do exception signals generalize across model architectures and scales?\n(2)~What is the relative contribution of each trace component?\nAll experiments use $N{=}1{,}190$ test samples with Wilson 95\\% CIs.\\footnote{Data is partitioned at the problem level (472 train / 119 test problems, zero crossover; \\S\\ref{sec:dataset}). The \\cond{exception\\_only} format collapses passing solutions into identical rows (48.99\\% test duplicates; Appendix~\\ref{sec:dedup_stats}), but these arise from within-problem variants, not cross-set leakage; deduplication sensitivity analysis confirms 0.00pp delta across all 19 experimental conditions.}\nAll cross-tier differences are significant at $p{<}.0001$ (paired bootstrap, $B{=}10{,}000$) unless noted.\nUnder problem-level clustered bootstrap (effective $N{\\approx}119$), cross-tier gaps remain significant ($p{<}.001$); within-tier comparisons show partially overlapping CIs.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Comparison}\n\\label{sec:cross_model}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_model} compares four models spanning three architecture families across a 1.5B--13B parameter range.\n\n\\paragraph{Exception dominance across families.}\nUnder \\cond{exception\\_only}, all four models converge to ${\\geq}$99.33\\% across all seeds, with a cross-model range of just 0.50pp---the tightest of any condition.\nCodeLlama-13B reaches 100\\% on one seed, confirming that the two persistent errors on Qwen (both \\textit{GeneratorExit}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:irreducible}) are convergence artifacts rather than hard data limitations.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale invariance.}\nException accuracy is independent of model scale: the smallest model (1.5B) matches the largest (13B) on the same representation (Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_scale}).\nIn contrast, \\cond{no\\_trace} scales monotonically with capacity and exhibits the highest seed variance of any condition.\nException events eliminate both the scale gap and the instability.\n\n\\paragraph{VOTA robustness.}\nExcluding one training failure caused by tokenizer-induced truncation (Appendix~\\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}), VOTA achieves ${\\geq}$98.57\\% across all models within a 0.80pp range.\nThe failure is itself informative: 91.7\\% of that seed's training samples were truncated, while compact \\cond{exception\\_only} inputs (median 150 tokens) sidestep truncation entirely.\n\n\\subsection{Component Ablation}\n\\label{sec:ablation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:ablation} decomposes VOTA on Qwen-2.5-7B, revealing a two-tier hierarchy.\nTier~1 signals are individually sufficient: \\cond{exception\\_only} alone exceeds full VOTA, implying that combining additional components introduces noise rather than value.\nRemoving assertion points has no effect (\\cond{VOTA\\_no\\_ae}), confirming exception dominance.\nThe Tier~1/2 boundary is sharp (Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot}): the weakest Tier~1 condition exceeds the strongest Tier~2 by over 12pp with non-overlapping confidence intervals.\nTier~2 (\\cond{loop\\_only}) benefits are model-dependent: Qwen-7B gains +10.92pp over \\cond{no\\_trace} while CodeLlama gains only +0.93pp (n.s.), suggesting loop utilization reflects architectural capacity to exploit structured iteration patterns rather than a universal signal.\nWithin Tier~2, the larger Qwen-7B outgains Qwen-1.5B (+10.92 vs.\\ +7.19pp, $p{<}.0001$), the only condition where scale provides a meaningful advantage.\n\n\\paragraph{Baselines.}\nThe strongest regex heuristic (exception class name matching) trails SFT \\cond{exception\\_only} by 6.22pp ($p{<}.001$); the gap concentrates on boundary cases where SFT distinguishes caught from uncaught exceptions, correctly resolving 6/8 ambiguous samples vs.\\ 0/8 for regex (Appendix~\\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).\nZero-shot prompting spans a 42pp range across models; SFT compresses this to 0.50pp with 4,720 training samples (Figure~\\ref{fig:zeroshot_vs_sft}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:zeroshot_cross_model}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{forest_plot_tier_boundary}\n\\caption{Cross-model accuracy with 95\\% Wilson CIs across all conditions and four models. The shaded region highlights the Tier~1/2 boundary gap ({>}12pp). Within Tier~1, all models converge to $\\geq$99.08\\%; within Tier~2, performance is model-dependent.}\n\\label{fig:forest_plot}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\\textwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{budget_curve}\n\\end{minipage}\\hfill\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\\textwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{token_distribution}\n\\end{minipage}\n\\caption{(\\textbf{Left}) Cross-model tail-truncation budget curve ($N{=}1{,}190$, four models). All models remain near baseline at tail-256/384, then jump sharply at 384$\\to$512 as the exception traceback is captured (shaded). Dashed: \\cond{exception\\_only} range; dotted: \\cond{no\\_trace} range. (\\textbf{Right}) Input token length distribution across trace conditions (training set, $N{=}4{,}720$). Dashed line: 2,048-token context window. \\cond{exception\\_only} inputs (median 150 tokens) fit entirely within the window, while \\cond{full\\_trace} inputs frequently exceed it, triggering truncation.}\n\\label{fig:trace_length}\n\\label{fig:token_distribution}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Format Marker Ablation}\n\\label{sec:nomarker}\n\nThe \\cond{exception\\_only} format includes a ``[No exception data in trace]'' marker for pass-class samples.\nRetraining with all format markers removed (\\cond{nomarker}) yields a modest drop (${\\sim}$2pp on both Qwen-7B and Qwen-1.5B), accounting for only ${\\sim}$9\\% of the total exception lift over \\cond{no\\_trace}.\nException events thus provide two signal layers: structural markers (${\\sim}$2pp, regex-learnable) and semantic content (${\\sim}$22pp, requiring SFT to distinguish caught from uncaught exceptions).\nBoth layers show no degradation at smaller scale.\n\n\\subsection{Information Density Decomposition}\n\\label{sec:density}\n\nThe gap between \\cond{full\\_trace} and VOTA decomposes via \\cond{full\\_trace\\_label\\_only}, which retains the full trace input but masks all trace tokens from the loss (Table~\\ref{tab:density}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Information density decomposition on Qwen-7B. Gradient masking (\\cond{label\\_only}) places an upper bound on backward-pass dilution; the remaining gap reflects inference-time input factors. 3-seed means.}\n\\label{tab:density}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ vs.\\ full\\_trace} & \\textbf{Gap explained} \\\\\n\\midrule\nfull\\_trace & 81.76 & --- & --- \\\\\nlabel\\_only & 85.94{\\scriptsize$\\pm$1.12} & +4.18pp & 24\\% \\\\\nVOTA & 99.05{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.38} & +17.29pp & 100\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\nGradient masking places an upper bound on backward-pass dilution: the +4.18pp gain bounds the backward-pass contribution to ${\\leq}$24\\% of the full gap.\nThe remaining majority of the gap persists even with identical loss computation, suggesting that interference from thousands of irrelevant trace lines at inference---rather than training signal dilution---is the dominant factor.\nCross-model evidence corroborates this: the tail-truncation phase transition at ${\\sim}$500 tokens (\\S\\ref{sec:trace_length}) coincides with exception traceback length, and cross-scale validation on Qwen-1.5B yields a consistent pattern (gradient masking explains a similar minority share).\nCodeLlama and DeepSeek exhibit label-only collapse, limiting the decomposition to models whose tokenizers preserve sufficient trace structure.\nError analysis confirms all \\cond{full\\_trace} errors occur where truncation removes diagnostic markers (Figure~\\ref{fig:token_distribution}).\nThe two factors may interact, and the decomposition is approximate (Qwen family, 2 scales, 3 seeds).\n\n\\subsection{Additional Analyses}\n\\label{sec:additional}\n\n\\paragraph{Tail-truncation budget curve.}\n\\label{sec:trace_length}\nA cross-model budget sweep reveals a sharp phase transition between 384 and 512 tail tokens (Figure~\\ref{fig:trace_length}).\nBelow this threshold, all models remain near baseline regardless of how many tokens are retained.\nAbove it, accuracy jumps by 13--22pp in a single step, converging into a narrow band by tail-1024.\nThe transition zone coincides with the typical exception traceback length (${\\sim}$400--500 tokens), providing spatial evidence that diagnostic events in the trace tail drive the accuracy gains.\n\nTail-truncation dramatically outperforms head-truncation: retaining the last $k$ tokens exceeds retaining the first $k$ by 15--23pp across models.\nUnder head-truncation, accuracy is non-monotonic and confined to a narrow band, because mid-length prefixes retain enough irrelevant content to mislead the model while discarding diagnostic events near the trace terminus (Appendix~\\ref{sec:trace_length_full}).\n\n\\paragraph{Calibration.}\n\\label{sec:calibration}\nCalibration mirrors the tier hierarchy: \\cond{exception\\_only} achieves ECE\\,=\\,0.003 vs.\\ 0.171 for \\cond{no\\_trace}, a 63$\\times$ gap (Figure~\\ref{fig:calibration}).\nThis advantage generalizes across all four models (ECE~${<}$~0.007; Appendix~\\ref{sec:calibration_full}).\n\n\\paragraph{Error orthogonality and exception types.}\n\\cond{exception\\_only}'s residual errors have zero overlap with all other conditions' error sets (Jaccard\\,=\\,0.000; Figure~\\ref{fig:error_overlap}), which confirms it operates on an independent signal dimension.\nAll standard exception types achieve 100\\% accuracy; the only failures arise from \\textit{GeneratorExit}, a benign \\textit{BaseException} subclass (Appendix~\\ref{sec:exception_type}).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-dataset generalization.}\n\\label{sec:debugbench}\nDebugBench \\citep{tian2024debugbench} ($N{=}426$) preserves the component ranking (\\cond{exception\\_only} 100\\%, VOTA 99.77\\%, \\cond{no\\_trace} 96.71\\%), though narrow margins reflect a ceiling effect from structurally distinctive mutations (Appendix~\\ref{sec:debugbench_full}).\n\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\nWe evaluate two questions:\n(1)~Do exception signals generalize across model architectures and scales?\n(2)~What is the relative contribution of each trace component?\nAll experiments use $N{=}1{,}190$ test samples with Wilson 95\\% CIs.\\footnote{Problem-level split (472/119 problems, zero crossover). Deduplication sensitivity analysis confirms 0.00pp delta across all 19 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{sec:dedup_stats}).}\nAll cross-tier differences are significant at $p{<}.0001$ (paired bootstrap, $B{=}10{,}000$) unless noted.\nUnder problem-level clustered bootstrap (effective $N{\\approx}119$), cross-tier gaps remain significant ($p{<}.001$); within-tier comparisons show partially overlapping CIs.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Comparison}\n\\label{sec:cross_model}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_model} compares four models spanning three architecture families across a 1.5B--13B parameter range.\n\n\\paragraph{Exception dominance across families.}\nUnder \\cond{exception\\_only}, all four models converge to ${\\geq}$99.33\\% across all seeds, with a cross-model range of just 0.50pp---the tightest of any condition.\nCodeLlama-13B reaches 100\\% on one seed, confirming that the two persistent errors on Qwen (both \\textit{GeneratorExit}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:irreducible}) are convergence artifacts rather than hard data limitations.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale invariance.}\nException accuracy is independent of model scale: the smallest model (1.5B) matches the largest (13B) on the same representation (Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_scale}).\nIn contrast, \\cond{no\\_trace} scales monotonically with capacity and exhibits the highest seed variance of any condition.\nException events eliminate both the scale gap and the instability.\n\n\\paragraph{VOTA robustness.}\nExcluding one training failure caused by tokenizer-induced truncation (Appendix~\\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}), VOTA achieves ${\\geq}$98.57\\% across all models within a 0.80pp range.\nThe failure is itself informative: 91.7\\% of that seed's training samples were truncated, while compact \\cond{exception\\_only} inputs (median 150 tokens) sidestep truncation entirely.\n\n\\subsection{Component Ablation}\n\\label{sec:ablation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:ablation} decomposes VOTA on Qwen-2.5-7B, revealing a two-tier hierarchy.\nTier~1 signals are individually sufficient: \\cond{exception\\_only} alone exceeds full VOTA, implying that combining additional components introduces noise rather than value.\nRemoving assertion points has no effect (\\cond{VOTA\\_no\\_ae}), confirming exception dominance.\nThe Tier~1/2 boundary is sharp (Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot}): the weakest Tier~1 condition exceeds the strongest Tier~2 by over 12pp with non-overlapping confidence intervals.\nTier~2 (\\cond{loop\\_only}) benefits are model-dependent: Qwen-7B gains +10.92pp over \\cond{no\\_trace} while CodeLlama gains only +0.93pp (n.s.), suggesting loop utilization reflects architectural capacity to exploit structured iteration patterns rather than a universal signal.\nWithin Tier~2, the larger Qwen-7B outgains Qwen-1.5B (+10.92 vs.\\ +7.19pp, $p{<}.0001$), the only condition where scale provides a meaningful advantage.\n\n\\paragraph{Baselines.}\nThe strongest regex heuristic (exception class name matching) trails SFT \\cond{exception\\_only} by 6.22pp ($p{<}.001$); the gap concentrates on boundary cases where SFT distinguishes caught from uncaught exceptions, correctly resolving 6/8 ambiguous samples vs.\\ 0/8 for regex (Appendix~\\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).\nZero-shot prompting spans a 42pp range across models; SFT compresses this to 0.50pp with 4,720 training samples (Figure~\\ref{fig:zeroshot_vs_sft}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:zeroshot_cross_model}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{forest_plot_tier_boundary}\n\\caption{Cross-model accuracy with 95\\% Wilson CIs across all conditions and four models. The shaded region highlights the Tier~1/2 boundary gap ({>}12pp). Within Tier~1, all models converge to $\\geq$99.08\\%; within Tier~2, performance is model-dependent.}\n\\label{fig:forest_plot}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\\textwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{budget_curve}\n\\end{minipage}\\hfill\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\\textwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{token_distribution}\n\\end{minipage}\n\\caption{(\\textbf{Left}) Cross-model tail-truncation budget curve ($N{=}1{,}190$, four models). All models remain near baseline at tail-256/384, then jump sharply at 384$\\to$512 as the exception traceback is captured (shaded). Dashed: \\cond{exception\\_only} range; dotted: \\cond{no\\_trace} range. (\\textbf{Right}) Input token length distribution across trace conditions (training set, $N{=}4{,}720$). Dashed line: 2,048-token context window. \\cond{exception\\_only} inputs (median 150 tokens) fit entirely within the window, while \\cond{full\\_trace} inputs frequently exceed it, triggering truncation.}\n\\label{fig:trace_length}\n\\label{fig:token_distribution}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Format Marker Ablation}\n\\label{sec:nomarker}\n\nThe \\cond{exception\\_only} format includes a ``[No exception data in trace]'' marker for pass-class samples.\nRetraining with all format markers removed (\\cond{nomarker}) yields a modest drop (${\\sim}$2pp on both Qwen-7B and Qwen-1.5B), accounting for only ${\\sim}$9\\% of the total exception lift over \\cond{no\\_trace}.\nException events thus provide two signal layers: structural markers (${\\sim}$2pp, regex-learnable) and semantic content (${\\sim}$22pp, requiring SFT to distinguish caught from uncaught exceptions).\nBoth layers show no degradation at smaller scale.\n\n\\subsection{Information Density Decomposition}\n\\label{sec:density}\n\nThe gap between \\cond{full\\_trace} and VOTA decomposes via \\cond{full\\_trace\\_label\\_only}, which retains the full trace input but masks all trace tokens from the loss (Table~\\ref{tab:density}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Information density decomposition on Qwen-7B. Gradient masking (\\cond{label\\_only}) places an upper bound on backward-pass dilution; the remaining gap reflects inference-time input factors. 3-seed means.}\n\\label{tab:density}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ vs.\\ full\\_trace} & \\textbf{Gap explained} \\\\\n\\midrule\nfull\\_trace & 81.76 & --- & --- \\\\\nlabel\\_only & 85.94{\\scriptsize$\\pm$1.12} & +4.18pp & 24\\% \\\\\nVOTA & 99.05{\\scriptsize$\\pm$0.38} & +17.29pp & 100\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\nGradient masking places an upper bound on backward-pass dilution: the +4.18pp gain bounds the backward-pass contribution to ${\\leq}$24\\% of the full gap.\nThe remaining majority of the gap persists even with identical loss computation, suggesting that interference from thousands of irrelevant trace lines at inference---rather than training signal dilution---is the dominant factor.\nCross-model evidence corroborates this: the tail-truncation phase transition at ${\\sim}$500 tokens (\\S\\ref{sec:trace_length}) coincides with exception traceback length, and cross-scale validation on Qwen-1.5B yields a consistent pattern (gradient masking explains a similar minority share).\nCodeLlama and DeepSeek exhibit label-only collapse, limiting the decomposition to models whose tokenizers preserve sufficient trace structure.\nError analysis confirms all \\cond{full\\_trace} errors occur where truncation removes diagnostic markers (Figure~\\ref{fig:token_distribution}).\nThe two factors may interact, and the decomposition is approximate (Qwen family, 2 scales, 3 seeds).\n\n\\subsection{Additional Analyses}\n\\label{sec:additional}\n\n\\paragraph{Tail-truncation budget curve.}\n\\label{sec:trace_length}\nA cross-model budget sweep reveals a sharp phase transition between 384 and 512 tail tokens (Figure~\\ref{fig:trace_length}).\nBelow this threshold, all models remain near baseline regardless of how many tokens are retained.\nAbove it, accuracy jumps by 13--22pp in a single step, converging into a narrow band by tail-1024.\nThe transition zone coincides with the typical exception traceback length (${\\sim}$400--500 tokens), providing spatial evidence that diagnostic events in the trace tail drive the accuracy gains.\n\nTail-truncation dramatically outperforms head-truncation: retaining the last $k$ tokens exceeds retaining the first $k$ by 15--23pp across models.\nUnder head-truncation, accuracy is non-monotonic and confined to a narrow band, because mid-length prefixes retain enough irrelevant content to mislead the model while discarding diagnostic events near the trace terminus (Appendix~\\ref{sec:trace_length_full}).\n\n\\paragraph{Calibration.}\n\\label{sec:calibration}\nCalibration mirrors the tier hierarchy: \\cond{exception\\_only} achieves ECE\\,=\\,0.003 vs.\\ 0.171 for \\cond{no\\_trace}, a 63$\\times$ gap (Figure~\\ref{fig:calibration}).\nThis advantage generalizes across all four models (ECE~${<}$~0.007; Appendix~\\ref{sec:calibration_full}).\n\n\\paragraph{Error orthogonality and exception types.}\n\\cond{exception\\_only}'s residual errors have zero overlap with all other conditions' error sets (Jaccard\\,=\\,0.000; Figure~\\ref{fig:error_overlap}), which confirms it operates on an independent signal dimension.\nAll standard exception types achieve 100\\% accuracy; the only failures arise from \\textit{GeneratorExit}, a benign \\textit{BaseException} subclass (Appendix~\\ref{sec:exception_type}).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-dataset generalization.}\n\\label{sec:debugbench}\nDebugBench \\citep{tian2024debugbench} ($N{=}426$) preserves the component ranking (\\cond{exception\\_only} 100\\%, VOTA 99.77\\%, \\cond{no\\_trace} 96.71\\%), though narrow margins reflect a ceiling effect from structurally distinctive mutations (Appendix~\\ref{sec:debugbench_full}).\n\n",
